Global Solutions of the Compressible Euler-Poisson Equations with Large Initial Data of Spherical Symmetry Volume: 000
Gui-Qiang G. Chen, Lin He, Yong Wang, Difan Yuan

Abstract
We are concerned with a global existence theory for finite-energy solutions of the multidimensional Euler-Poisson equations for both compressible gaseous stars and plasmas with large initial data of spherical symmetry. One of the main challenges is the strengthening of waves as they move radially inward towards the origin, especially under the self-consistent gravitational field for gaseous stars. A fundamental unsolved problem is whether the density of the global solution forms a delta measure ( i.e. , concentration) at the origin. To solve this problem, we develop a new approach for the construction of approximate solutions as the solutions of an appropriately formulated free boundary problem for the compressible Navier-Stokes-Poisson equations with a carefully adapted class of degenerate density-dependent viscosity terms, so that a rigorous convergence proof of the approximate solutions to the corresponding global solution of the compressible Euler-Poisson equations with large initial data of spherical symmetry can be obtained. Even though the density may blow up near the origin at a certain time, it is proved that no delta measure ( i.e. , concentration) in space-time is formed in the vanishing viscosity limit for the finite-energy solutions of the compressible Euler-Poisson equations for both gaseous stars and plasmas in the physical regimes under consideration.

\authorheadline
Chen G.-Q. et al

\titleheadline
Global Solutions of the Compressible Euler-Poisson Equations

University of Oxford, Oxford UK

School of Mathematics, Sichuan University, Chengdu, China

Academy of Mathematics and Systems Science
Chinese Academy of Sciences, Beijing, China

School of Mathematical Sciences, Beijing Normal University, Beijing, China

1
Introduction

We are concerned with the global existence theory for spherically symmetric solutions of the
multidimensional (M-D) compressible Euler-Poisson equations (CEPEs) with large initial data.
CEPEs govern the motion of compressible gaseous stars or plasmas under a self-consistent
gravitational field
or an electric field,
which take the form:

(1.1)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\partial_{t}\rho+\mbox{div}{\mathcal{M}}=0,\\[2.84526pt]
\partial_{t}{\mathcal{M}}+\mbox{div}\big(\frac{{\mathcal{M}}\otimes{\mathcal{M}}}{\rho}\big)+\nabla p+\rho\nabla\Phi=\mathbf{0},\\
\Delta\Phi=\kappa\rho,\end{cases}

for
t>0
,
\textbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, and
n\geq 3
,
where
\rho
is the density,
p
is the pressure,
{\mathcal{M}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
represents the momentum,
and
\Phi
represents the gravitational potential of gaseous stars if
\kappa>0

and the plasma electric field potential if
\kappa<0
.
When
\rho>0
,
U=\frac{{\mathcal{M}}}{\rho}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
is the velocity.
By scaling, we always fix
\kappa=\pm 1
throughout this paper; that is,

\kappa=1
for the gaseous star and
\kappa=-1
for the plasma.
The pressure-density relation is

p=p(\rho)=a_{0}\rho^{\gamma},

where
\gamma>1
is the adiabatic exponent.
Again, by scaling, constant
a_{0}>0
may be chosen to be
a_{0}=\frac{(\gamma-1)^{2}}{4\gamma}
.

We consider the Cauchy problem for (
1.1
) with the Cauchy data:

(1.2)

(\rho,{\mathcal{M}})|_{t=0}=(\rho_{0},{\mathcal{M}}_{0})(\mathbf{x})\longrightarrow(0,\mathbf{0})\qquad\,\,\,\,\mbox{as $|\mathbf{x}|\to\infty$},

subject to the asymptotic condition:

(1.3)

\Phi(t,\mathbf{x})\longrightarrow 0\qquad\,\,\,\,\mbox{as $|\mathbf{x}|\to\infty$}.

In (
1.2
), the initial far-field velocity has been assumed to be zero in (
1.2
)
without loss of generality, owing to the Galilean invariance of system (
1.1
).
Since a global solution of CEPEs (
1.1
) normally contains the vacuum states

\{(t,\mathbf{x})\,:\,\rho(t,\mathbf{x})=0\}
where
the fluid velocity
U(t,\mathbf{x})
is not well-defined,
we use the physical variables such as the momentum

{\mathcal{M}}(t,\mathbf{x})
, or
\frac{{\mathcal{M}}(t,\mathbf{x})}{\sqrt{\rho(t,\mathbf{x})}}

(which will be shown to be always well-defined globally), instead of
U(t,\mathbf{x})
,
when the vacuum states are involved.

The global existence for problem (
1.1
)–(
1.3
) is
challenging, mainly owing to the possible appearance of cavitation and concentration,
besides the formation of shock waves, in the solutions,
which leads to the lack of higher order regularity of the solutions, so
that our main focus has to be finite-energy solutions for CEPEs (
1.1
).
To solve this existence problem,
we consider the vanishing viscosity limit of the solutions of
the compressible Navier-Stokes-Poisson equations (CNSPEs)
with carefully adapted density-dependent viscosity terms in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
:

(1.4)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\partial_{t}\rho+\mbox{div}{\mathcal{M}}=0,\\[2.84526pt]
\partial_{t}{\mathcal{M}}+\mbox{div}\big(\frac{{\mathcal{M}}\otimes{\mathcal{M}}}{\rho}\big)+\nabla p+\rho\nabla\Phi=\varepsilon\mbox{div}\big(\mu(\rho)D(\frac{{\mathcal{M}}}{\rho})\big)+\varepsilon\nabla\big(\lambda(\rho)\,\mbox{div}(\frac{{\mathcal{M}}}{\rho})\big),\\
\Delta\Phi=\kappa\rho,\end{cases}

where
D(\frac{{\mathcal{M}}}{\rho})=\frac{1}{2}\big(\nabla(\frac{{\mathcal{M}}}{\rho})+(\nabla(\frac{{\mathcal{M}}}{\rho}))^{\top}\big)
is the stress tensor,
the Lamé (shear and bulk) viscosity coefficients
\mu(\rho)
and
\lambda(\rho)
depend on the density
(that may vanish on the vacuum) and satisfy

\mu(\rho)\geq 0,\quad\mu(\rho)+n\lambda(\rho)\geq 0\qquad\,\,\,\mbox{for $\rho\geq 0$},

and parameter
\varepsilon>0
is the inverse of the Reynolds number.
Formally, as
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0+
, CNSPEs (
1.4
) converge to
CEPEs (
1.1
). However, its rigorous mathematical proof has been one of the most challenging open
problems in mathematical fluid dynamics;
see Chen-Feldman
[
7
]
, Dafermos
[
15
]
, and the references cited therein.

Many efforts have been made in the analysis of CEPEs (
1.1
). We focus mainly on
some relevant time-dependent problems.
Some important progress has been made on the M-D CEPEs with
\kappa=-1
(plasmas)
in Guo
[
26
]
, Guo-Ionescu-Pausader
[
29
]
, Guo-Pausader
[
30
]
,
and Ionescu-Pausader
[
38
]
,
in which they proved the global existence of smooth solutions around a constant neutral background
under irrotational, smooth, and localized perturbations of the background with small amplitude.
For the
3
-D gaseous stars problem
(\kappa=1)
,
a compactly supported expanding
classical solution was discovered by Goldreich-Weber
[
24
]
in 1980;
see also
[
22
,
59
]
.
Hadzic-Jang
[
32
]
proved the nonlinear stability of the Goldreich-Weber
solution under small spherically symmetric perturbations for the adiabatic exponent
\gamma=\frac{4}{3}
,
while the problem for
\gamma\neq\frac{4}{3}
is still widely open.
When the initial density is small and has compact support,
Hadzic-Jang
[
33
]
constructed a class of global-in-time solutions
of the 3-D CEPEs in the Lagrangian coordinates for
\gamma=1+\frac{1}{k}
with
k\in\mathbb{N}\backslash\{1\}
,
or
\gamma\in(1,\frac{14}{13})
.
More recently, Guo-Hadzic-Jang
[
27
]
constructed an infinite-D
family of spherically symmetric collapsing solutions of the 3-D CEPEs (
1.1
) for
\gamma\in(1,\frac{4}{3})
,
that is, the gas star continuously shrinks to be one point (
i.e.
, delta measure);
see
[
28
]
for the case that
\gamma=1
.
We also refer
[
46
,
56
,
58
]
for the local
well-posedness of smooth solutions.

On the other hand, owing to the strong hyperbolicity and nonlinearity,
the smooth solutions of (
1.1
) may break down in finite time, especially
when the initial data are large (
cf.

[
11
,
59
]
).
Therefore, the weak solutions have to be considered for the Cauchy problem with large initial data.
For gaseous stars (
i.e.
,
\kappa=1
) surrounding a solid ball,
Makino
[
60
]
proved the local existence of
weak solutions for
\gamma\in(1,\frac{5}{3}]
with spherical symmetry;
also see Xiao
[
74
]
for global weak solutions
for a class of initial data.
For the compressible Euler equations,
we refer to

[
6
,
8
,
10
,
12
,
13
,
16
,
17
,
18
,
34
,
40
,
45
,
52
,
53
]

and the references therein.

For CNSPEs (
1.4
), many efforts have also been made regarding the global existence of solutions.
For CNSPEs (
1.4
) with constant viscosity,
some global existence results for weak solutions for viscous gaseous stars (
i.e.
,
\kappa=1
)
have been obtained;
see also
[
20
,
39
,
42
,
43
,
57
]

and the references cited therein.
For CNSPEs with density-dependent viscosity terms,
Zhang-Fang
[
76
]
obtained a unique global weak solution for
a spherically symmetric vacuum free boundary problem with
\gamma>1

for a small perturbation around some steady solution;
the global existence of spherically symmetric weak solutions was proved by Duan-Li
[
19
]

for the
3
-D problem for
\kappa=1
and
\gamma\in(\frac{6}{5},\frac{4}{3}]

with stress free boundary condition and nonzero initial density
for arbitrarily large initial data.
Recently, Luo-Xin-Zeng
[
54
,
55
]
proved
the existence and large-time stability of spherically symmetric smooth solutions of
the
3
-D viscous problem (with
\kappa=1
) for a small perturbation around the
Lane-Emden solution for
\gamma\in(\frac{4}{3},2)
.
For the global existence of solutions of the compressible Navier-Stokes equations,
we refer to
[
21
,
35
,
41
,
51
,
61
]

for the case with constant viscosity,

[
47
,
72
]
for the case with density-dependent viscosity,
and the references cited therein.
In particular, we remark that
the BD entropy estimate
developed in
[
4
]
for the derivative estimate of the density
plays a key role in
[
47
,
72
]
.
Such an estimate is based on the new mathematical entropy – the BD entropy,
first discovered by Bresch-Desjardins
[
1
]
for the particular case
(\mu,\lambda)=(\rho,0)
,
and later
generalized by Bresch-Desjardins
[
2
]
to include any viscosity
coefficients
(\mu,\lambda)
satisfying the BD relation:

\lambda(\rho)=\rho\mu^{\prime}(\rho)-\mu(\rho)
;
also see
[
3
]
.
The BD-type entropy will also be used in this paper.

The idea of regarding inviscid gases as viscous gases with vanishing physical
viscosity can date back to the seminal paper by Stokes
[
70
]
;
see also the important contributions in
[
15
,
37
,
65
,
66
]
.
Most of the known results are for the vanishing viscosity limit from the compressible Navier-Stokes
to Euler equations.
The first rigorous convergence analysis of the vanishing physical viscosity limit
from the barotropic Navier-Stokes to Euler equations
was made by Gilbarg
[
23
]
, in which he established the mathematical existence
and vanishing viscosity limit of the Navier-Stokes shock layers.
For the convergence analysis confined in the framework of piecewise smooth solutions;
see
[
25
,
36
,
75
]
and the references cited therein.
For general data, due to the lack of
L^{\infty}
uniform estimate,
the
L^{\infty}
compensated compactness framework
[
16
,
17
,
18
,
52
,
53
]

does not apply directly for the vanishing viscosity limit of the compressible Navier-Stokes equations.
LeFloch-Westdickenberg
[
45
]
first developed an
L^{p}
compensated compactness framework
for approximate solutions of the isentropic Euler equations for the adiabatic exponent
\gamma\in(1,\frac{5}{3})
.
In order to establish the vanishing viscosity limit as discussed above,
Chen-Perepelitsa
[
8
]
generalized the
L^{p}
compensated compactness framework, especially including the whole physical range of adiabatic exponent
\gamma>1
, by further developing/simplifying the proof arguments
and then applied it to establish rigorously the vanishing viscosity limit
of the solutions of the
1
-D compressible Navier-Stokes equations to
the corresponding relative finite-energy solutions of the Euler equations
for large initial data.
Most recently, Chen-Wang
[
12
]
established the vanishing viscosity limit of the compressible Navier-Stokes
equations with general data of spherical symmetry and obtained the global existence
of spherically symmetric solutions of the compressible Euler equations with large initial data,
in which it was proved that no delta measure is formed for the density function at the origin.

For problem (
1.1
)–(
1.3
), owing to
the additional difficulties arisen from the possible appearance of concentration and cavitation,
besides the involvement of shock waves,
it has been a longstanding open problem to construct global finite-energy solutions
with large initial data of spherical symmetry.
The key objective of this paper is to solve this problem and establish the global existence of
spherically symmetric finite-energy solutions of (
1.1
):

(1.5)

\displaystyle\rho(t,\mathbf{x})=\rho(t,r),\,\,\,\,{\mathcal{M}}(t,\mathbf{x})=m(t,r)\frac{\mathbf{x}}{r},\,\,\,\,\Phi(t,\mathbf{x})=\Phi(t,r)\qquad\mbox{for $r=|\mathbf{x}|$},

subject to the initial condition:

(1.6)

\displaystyle(\rho,{\mathcal{M}})(0,\mathbf{x})=(\rho_{0},{\mathcal{M}}_{0})(\mathbf{x})=(\rho_{0}(r),m_{0}(r)\frac{\mathbf{x}}{r})\longrightarrow(0,\mathbf{0})\qquad\mbox{as $r\to\infty$},

and the asymptotic boundary condition:

(1.7)

\displaystyle\Phi(t,\mathbf{x})=\Phi(t,r)\longrightarrow 0\qquad\mbox{as $r\to\infty$}.

Since
\Phi(0,\mathbf{x})
can be determined by the initial density in (
1.6
)
and the boundary condition in (
1.7
),
there is no need to impose initial data for
\Phi
.

To achieve this, we establish the vanishing viscosity limit of the corresponding spherically symmetric
solutions of CNSPEs (
1.4
) with the
adapted class of degenerate density-dependent viscosity terms and
approximate initial data of similar form to
(
1.6
).
For spherically symmetric solutions of form (
1.5
),
systems (
1.1
) and (
1.4
) become

(1.8)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\displaystyle\rho_{t}+m_{r}+\frac{n-1}{r}m=0,\\[2.84526pt]
\displaystyle m_{t}+\big(\frac{m^{2}}{\rho}+p\big)_{r}+\frac{n-1}{r}\frac{m^{2}}{\rho}+\rho\Phi_{r}=0,\\[2.84526pt]
\displaystyle\Phi_{rr}+\frac{n-1}{r}\Phi_{r}=\kappa\rho,\end{cases}

and

(1.9)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\displaystyle\rho_{t}+m_{r}+\frac{n-1}{r}m=0,\\[2.84526pt]
\displaystyle m_{t}+\big(\frac{m^{2}}{\rho}+p\big)_{r}+\frac{n-1}{r}\frac{m^{2}}{\rho}+\rho\Phi_{r}=\varepsilon\Big((\mu+\lambda)\big((\frac{m}{\rho})_{r}+\frac{n-1}{r}\frac{m}{\rho}\big)\Big)_{r}-\varepsilon\frac{n-1}{r}\frac{m}{\rho}\mu_{r},\\[2.84526pt]
\displaystyle\Phi_{rr}+\frac{n-1}{r}\Phi_{r}=\kappa\rho,\end{cases}

respectively.

The study of spherically symmetric solutions can date back to the 1950s
and has been motivated by many important physical problems such as stellar dynamics including gaseous stars
and supernova formation
[
5
,
67
,
73
]
.
In fact, the most famous solutions of CEPEs (
1.1
) are the Lane-Emden steady solutions
[
5
,
50
]
,
which describe spherically symmetric gaseous stars in equilibrium and minimize the energy among all possible configurations.
More precisely, for the
3
-D case, there exists a compactly supported and spherically symmetric steady solution
with finite mass for
\gamma\in(\frac{6}{5},2)
.
For the time-dependent system, the central feature is the strengthening of waves as they move radially inward near the origin,
especially under the self-gravitational force for gaseous stars.
The spherically symmetric solutions of the compressible Euler equations may blow up near
the origin
[
14
,
48
,
63
,
73
]
at a certain time in some situations.
However, it has not been well understood how the spherically symmetric solutions of CEPEs (
1.1
) with
self-gravitational force (which drags the gas particles to the origin) blow up when the initial total-energy is finite.
A fundamental unsolved problem is whether a concentration is formed at the origin;
that is, the density becomes a delta measure at the origin, especially when a focusing spherical shock
is moving inward towards the origin under self-consistent gravitational field.

In this paper, we establish the global existence of finite-energy solutions of problem (
1.1
)–(
1.3
) for CEPEs
with spherical symmetry as the vanishing viscosity limits of global weak solutions of
CNSPEs (
1.4
) with corresponding initial and asymptotic conditions,
which indicates that no delta measure is formed for the density of the solution of problem (
1.1
)–(
1.3
)
in the limit indeed.
To achieve these,
the main point is to establish appropriate uniform estimates in
L^{p}
and the
H^{-1}_{\rm loc}
–compactness of the entropy dissipation measures
for the solutions of CNSPEs (
1.9
) subject to the corresponding initial and asymptotic conditions.
Owing to the possible appearance of cavitation, the singularity of geometric source terms at the origin, as well as the gravitational force for the gaseous star case,
the global solutions of CNSPEs (
1.9
) with large initial data are not smooth in general.
Thus, we start with
the construction of approximate smooth solutions of
the truncated approximate problem (
3.1
)–(
3.6
) for CNSPEs (
1.4
),
where the origin and the far-field are cut off, and a stress-free boundary condition is imposed.

In general, we have two basic candidates for the boundary conditions of the approximate problem (
3.1
)–(
3.6
):
One is to use the Dirichlet boundary conditions:
u(t,a)=u(t,b)=0
as in
[
12
]
, in which case it is difficult
to obtain the higher integrability on the velocity (see Lemma
3.7
) due to the far-field vacuum (since the total mass is finite).
Another choice is to use the vacuum free boundary condition; however the global existence of smooth solutions
with the vacuum free boundary condition and large initial data is still an important open problem.
One of our main observations is that the stress-free boundary conditions (
3.3
)–(
3.4
) we have adapted in §3
serve our purpose to avoid the difficulties mentioned above.
Even though, we still have to overcome the following additional difficulties:

(i)

Owing to the effect of self-gravitational force for
\kappa=1
,
we need condition (
2.8
) in §2 to close the basic energy estimate
for
\gamma\in(\frac{2n}{n+2},\frac{2(n-1)}{n}]
, which implies that
the initial total mass
can not be too large when the total initial-energy is fixed.
The lower bound condition
\gamma>\frac{2n}{2+n}
is essentially used when we deal with the gravitational potential.

(ii)

To obtain the derivative estimate of the density, we use the BD entropy introduced
in
[
1
,
4
]
; also see
[
2
,
3
]
.
To close the bound, we need to control the boundary
term involving
p(\rho_{0}^{\varepsilon,b}(b))b^{n}
for the approximate initial data;
see (
3.56
).
To solve this problem, we construct the approximate initial data
(\rho_{0}^{\varepsilon,b},\rho_{0}^{\varepsilon,b}u_{0}^{\varepsilon,b})

so that
p(\rho_{0}^{\varepsilon,b}(b))b^{n}

are uniformly bounded; see (
3.12
).
The details of constructing the approximate initial data are given in Appendix A; see Lemmas
A.2
–
A.10
.

(iii)

For the
free boundary problem (
3.1
)–(
3.6
) below,
a follow-up point is whether the free boundary domain
\Omega_{T}
(see (
3.2
))
will expand to the whole space as
b\rightarrow\infty
;
otherwise, it would not be a good approximation to the original Cauchy problem.
We deal with this difficulty by proving that

(1.10)

b(t)\geq\frac{b}{2}\qquad\mbox{for $\,t\in[0,T]$},

provided
b\gg 1
for any given
T
.
Property (
3.12
) of the constructed approximate initial density
is crucial to prove (
1.10
); see Lemma
3.4
for details.

(iv)

To utilize the
L^{p}
compensated compactness framework
[
8
]
,
we still need to have the higher velocity integrability.
We use the entropy pairs
(\eta^{\#},q^{\#})
generated by
\psi(s)=\frac{1}{2}s|s|
.
Then we have to deal with the boundary term
(q^{\#}-u\eta^{\#})(t,b(t))b(t)^{n-1}
.
In general, it is impossible to have a uniform bound for both
q^{\#}(t,b(t))b(t)^{n-1}
and
(u\eta^{\#})(t,b(t))b(t)^{n-1}
.
One of our key observations is the cancellation between
q^{\#}(t,b(t))
and
(u\eta^{\#})(t,b(t))
via observing that

(1.11)

\displaystyle|q^{\#}-u\eta^{\#}|\leq C\big(\rho^{\gamma}|u|+\rho^{\gamma+\theta}\big).

With the help of the trace estimates in the basic estimates and the BD entropy estimate,
it serves perfectly to obtain the uniform trace estimate for the terms on the right-hand side of (
1.11
).
On the other hand, the trace of
u_{r}
can be handled by using (
3.4
); see (
3.106
) for details.

This paper is organized as follows:
In §2, we first introduce the notion of finite-energy solutions of
problem (
1.1
)–(
1.3
) for CEPEs
and then state the main theorems of this paper and several remarks.
In §3, we first derive some uniform estimates of
the solutions of the free boundary problem (
3.1
)–(
3.6
)
for the approximate CNSPEs.
In §4, we establish the global existence of weak finite-energy solutions of (
1.4
) with large initial data of spherical symmetry and finite-energy.
Moreover, some uniform estimates in
L^{p}
and the
H_{\rm loc}^{-1}
–compactness of entropy dissipation measures for the weak solutions
of CNSPEs (
1.9
) are also obtained.
In §5, the vanishing viscosity limit of weak solutions of CNSPEs (
1.9
) is proved by using the compensated compactness
framework
[
8
]
, which leads to a global finite-energy solution of CEPEs (
1.1
).
In Appendix A, we construct the approximate initial data with desired properties.

Throughout this paper,
we denote
L^{p}(\Omega),W^{k,p}(\Omega)
, and
H^{k}(\Omega)
as the standard Sobolev
space on domain
\Omega
for
p\in[1,\infty]
.
We also use
L^{p}(I;\,r^{n-1}{\rm d}r)
or
L^{p}([0,T)\times I;\,r^{n-1}{\rm d}r{\rm d}t)

for open interval
I\subset\mathbb{R}_{+}
with measure
r^{n-1}{\rm d}r\,
or
r^{n-1}{\rm d}r{\rm d}t\,
correspondingly, and

L^{p}_{\rm loc}([0,\infty);r^{n-1}{\rm d}r)
to represent
L^{p}([0,R);\,r^{n-1}{\rm d}r)
for any fixed
R>0
.

2
Mathematical Problem and Main Theorems

In this section, we first introduce the notion of finite-energy solutions
of problem (
1.1
)–(
1.3
) for CEPEs
in
\mathbb{R}_{+}^{n+1}:=\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}=[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
for
n\geq 3
.

We assume that the initial data
(\rho_{0},{\mathcal{M}}_{0})(\mathbf{x})
and corresponding initial potential function
\Phi_{0}(\mathbf{x})

have both finite initial total-energy:

(2.1)

\displaystyle E_{0}:=\begin{cases}\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\Big(\frac{1}{2}\big|\frac{{\mathcal{M}}_{0}}{\sqrt{\rho_{0}}}\big|^{2}+\rho_{0}e(\rho_{0})+\frac{1}{2}|\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}\Phi_{0}|^{2}\Big)(\mathbf{x})\,{\rm d}\mathbf{x}<\infty&\mbox{for $\kappa=-1$ $($plasmas$)$},\\[11.38109pt]
\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\Big(\frac{1}{2}\big|\frac{{\mathcal{M}}_{0}}{\sqrt{\rho_{0}}}\big|^{2}+\rho_{0}e(\rho_{0})\Big)(\mathbf{x})\,{\rm d}\mathbf{x}<\infty\,\,&\hskip-14.22636pt\mbox{for $\kappa=1$ $($gaseous stars$)$},\end{cases}

and finite initial total-mass:

(2.2)

M:=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\rho_{0}(\mathbf{x})\,{\rm d}\mathbf{x}=\omega_{n}\int_{0}^{\infty}\rho_{0}(r)\,r^{n-1}{\rm d}r<\infty,

where
e(\rho):=\frac{a_{0}}{\gamma-1}\rho^{\gamma-1}
represents the internal energy,
and

\omega_{n}:=\frac{2\pi^{\frac{n}{2}}}{\Gamma(\frac{n}{2})}
denotes the surface area
of the unit sphere in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

Definition 2.1
.

A measurable vector function

(\rho,{\mathcal{M}},\Phi)
is said to be a finite-energy solution of the Cauchy problem (
1.1
)–(
1.3
)
if the following conditions hold:

(i)

\rho(t,\mathbf{x})\geq 0\,\,\,

a.e.
,    and
\,\,\,(\mathcal{M},\frac{\mathcal{M}}{\sqrt{\rho}})(t,\mathbf{x})=\mathbf{0}\,\,

a.e.
on
the   vacuum   states
\,\{(t,\mathbf{x})\,:\,\rho(t,\mathbf{x})=0\}
.

(ii)

For a.e.
t>0
, the total energy is finite:

•

For
\kappa=-1

(
plasmas
)
,

(2.3)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\Big(\frac{1}{2}\big|\frac{{\mathcal{M}}}{\sqrt{\rho}}\big|^{2}+\rho e(\rho)+\frac{1}{2}|\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}\Phi|^{2}\Big)(t,\mathbf{x})\,{\rm d}\mathbf{x}\leq E_{0};

•

For
\kappa=1

(
gaseous stars
)
,

(2.4)

\displaystyle\quad\quad\begin{cases}\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\Big(\frac{1}{2}\big|\frac{{\mathcal{M}}}{\sqrt{\rho}}\big|^{2}+\rho e(\rho)+\frac{1}{2}|\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}\Phi|^{2}\Big)(t,\mathbf{x})\,{\rm d}\mathbf{x}\leq C(E_{0},M),\\[8.53581pt]
\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\Big(\frac{1}{2}\big|\frac{{\mathcal{M}}}{\sqrt{\rho}}\big|^{2}+\rho e(\rho)-\frac{1}{2}|\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}\Phi|^{2}\Big)(t,\mathbf{x})\,{\rm d}\mathbf{x}\\
\qquad\leq\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\Big(\frac{1}{2}\big|\frac{{\mathcal{M}}_{0}}{\sqrt{\rho_{0}}}\big|^{2}+\rho_{0}e(\rho_{0})-\frac{1}{2}|\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}\Phi_{0}|^{2}\Big)\,{\rm d}\mathbf{x}.\end{cases}

(iii)

For any
\zeta(t,\mathbf{x})\in C^{1}_{0}(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{n+1})
,

(2.5)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}\big(\rho\zeta_{t}+{\mathcal{M}}\cdot\nabla\zeta\big)\,{\rm d}\mathbf{x}{\rm d}t+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\rho_{0}(\mathbf{x})\zeta(0,\mathbf{x})\,{\rm d}\mathbf{x}=0.

(iv)

For any
\boldsymbol{\psi}(t,\mathbf{x})=(\psi_{1},\cdots,\psi_{n})(t,\mathbf{x})\in\big(C^{1}_{0}(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{n+1})\big)^{n}
,

(2.6)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}\Big({\mathcal{M}}\cdot\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{\psi}+\frac{{\mathcal{M}}}{\sqrt{\rho}}\cdot\big(\frac{{\mathcal{M}}}{\sqrt{\rho}}\cdot\nabla\big)\boldsymbol{\psi}+p(\rho)\,\mbox{\rm div}\,\boldsymbol{\psi}\Big)(t,\mathbf{x})\,{\rm d}\mathbf{x}{\rm d}t

\displaystyle+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}{\mathcal{M}}_{0}(\mathbf{x})\cdot\boldsymbol{\psi}(0,\mathbf{x})\,{\rm d}\mathbf{x}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}(\rho\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}\Phi\cdot\boldsymbol{\psi})(t,\mathbf{x})\,{\rm d}\mathbf{x}{\rm d}t.

(v)

For all
\xi(\mathbf{x})\in C_{0}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
,

(2.7)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}\Phi(t,\mathbf{x})\cdot\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}\xi(\mathbf{x})\,{\rm d}\mathbf{x}=-\kappa\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\rho(t,\mathbf{x})\,\xi(\mathbf{x})\,{\rm d}\mathbf{x}\qquad\mbox{for {\it a.e.} $t\geq 0$}.

For the case that
\kappa=1
(gaseous stars), denote
M_{\rm c}(\gamma)
as the critical mass given by

(2.8)

\displaystyle M_{\rm c}(\gamma):=\begin{cases}B_{n,\gamma}^{-\frac{n}{2}}\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad&\mbox{for $\gamma=\frac{2(n-1)}{n}$},\\[5.69054pt]
\left(\frac{(n-2)B_{n,\gamma}}{n(\gamma-1)}\right)^{-\frac{n(\gamma-1)}{(n+2)\gamma-2n}}\,\left(\frac{(n-2)E_{0}}{(2(n-1)-n\gamma)\omega_{n}}\right)^{-\frac{2(n-1)-n\gamma}{(n+2)\gamma-2n}}\quad&\mbox{for $\gamma\in(\frac{2n}{n+2},\frac{2(n-1)}{n})$},\end{cases}

with
\displaystyle B_{n,\gamma}:=\frac{2\kappa}{n(n-2)}\Big(\frac{a_{0}}{\gamma-1}\Big)^{-\frac{n-2}{n(\gamma-1)}}\,\omega_{n}^{\frac{2(n-1)-n\gamma}{n(\gamma-1)}}\,\omega_{n+1}^{-\frac{2}{n}}

depending only on
(n,\gamma)
.

We now state the main theorem of this paper.

Theorem 2.2

(Main Theorem I: Existence of spherically symmetric solutions of CEPEs)

Consider problem (
1.1
)–(
1.3
) for CEPEs with
large initial data of spherical symmetry of form (
1.6
)–(
1.7
).
Let
(\rho_{0},{\mathcal{M}}_{0},\Phi_{0})(\mathbf{x})
satisfy (
2.1
)–(
2.2
).
In addition,

(a)

when
\kappa=-1

(
plasmas
)
, assume that
\gamma>1
, and

\rho_{0}\in L^{\frac{2n}{n+2}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
when
\gamma\in(1,\frac{2n}{n+2});

(b)

when
\kappa=1

(
gaseous stars
)
,
assume that
\gamma>\frac{2(n-1)}{n}
, or
M<M_{\rm c}(\gamma)
when
\gamma\in(\frac{2n}{n+2},\frac{2(n-1)}{n}]
.

Then there exists a global finite-energy solution
(\rho,{\mathcal{M}},\Phi)(t,\mathbf{x})
of problem (
1.1
)–(
1.3
)
and (
1.6
)–(
1.7
) with
spherical symmetry of form (
1.5
) in the sense of Definition
2.1
,
where
(\rho,m,\Phi)(t,r)
is determined by
the corresponding
system (
1.8
) with initial data

(\rho_{0},m_{0},\Phi_{0})(r)
given in (
1.6
) subject to the asymptotic condition (
1.7
).

Remark 2.3
.

By the Poisson equation, the initial condition on
\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}\Phi_{0}
is indeed a condition on the initial density
\rho_{0}
.
In fact, to make the Poisson equation solvable and sense,
we need the additional condition
\rho_{0}\in(L^{\frac{2n}{n+2}}\cap L^{1})(\mathbb{R}^{n})
for case
\kappa=-1
(plasmas)
if
\gamma>1

as required in Theorem
2.2
.
However, for case
\kappa=1
(gaseous stars),
such an additional condition is not required for
\gamma>\frac{2n}{n+2}
.

Remark 2.4
.

To the best of our knowledge, Theorem
2.2
provides the first global-in-time solution of
the M-D CEPEs (
1.1
) with large initial data.
For
\kappa=1

(
gaseous stars
)
, condition
\gamma>\frac{2n}{n+2}
(
e.g.

\gamma>\frac{6}{5}
for
n=3
) is necessary
to ensure the global existence of finite-energy solutions with finite total mass,
which
corresponds to the one for the Lane-Emden solutions
(
cf
.
[
5
,
50
]
)
.
Moreover, it has been shown in
[
5
,
50
]
that there is no spherically symmetric steady solution
of gas-stars for the
3
-D CEPEs
when
\gamma\in(1,\frac{6}{5})
with finite total mass;
thus it has been conjectured that there is no global-in-time solution even in the weak sense in general.

Remark 2.5
.

For the steady gaseous star problem, it is well-known that there exists no steady white dwarf star with total mass
larger than the Chandrasekhar limit
M_{\rm ch}
when
\gamma\in(\frac{6}{5},\frac{4}{3}]
; see
[
5
]
.
In this paper, for the
3
-D time-dependent gaseous star problem with
\gamma\in(\frac{6}{5},\frac{4}{3}]
,
we also need the restriction on the total mass of the gaseous star:
M<M_{\rm c}(\gamma)
with
M_{c}(\gamma)
defined in (
2.8
),
which is consistent with the phenomenon observed by Chandrasekhar
[
5
]
.
It is interesting to clarify whether the delta measure could be formed at some time when
M<M_{\rm c}(\gamma)
is violated.
Indeed, when
\gamma\in(1,\frac{4}{3})
, Guo-Hadzic-Jang
[
27
]
recently constructed an infinite-D family of spherically
symmetric collapsing solutions of the
3
-D CEPEs (
1.1
);
that is, the gas star continuously shrinks to be one point
(
i.e.
, delta measure
)
; see
[
28
]

for the case that
\gamma=1
.

To establish Theorem
2.2
,
we first construct global weak solutions for CNSPEs (
1.4
)
with appropriately adapted degenerate density-dependent viscosity terms and approximate initial data:

(2.9)

(\rho,{\mathcal{M}},\Phi)|_{t=0}=(\rho_{0}^{\varepsilon},{\mathcal{M}}_{0}^{\varepsilon},\Phi_{0}^{\varepsilon})(\mathbf{x})\longrightarrow(\rho_{0},{\mathcal{M}}_{0},\Phi_{0})(\mathbf{x})\qquad\,\,\,\mbox{as $\varepsilon\to 0$},

constructed as in Appendix A satisfying Lemmas
A.2
–
A.10
, subject to the asymptotic boundary condition:

(2.10)

\Phi^{\varepsilon}(t,\mathbf{x})\longrightarrow 0\qquad\,\,\,\mbox{as $|\mathbf{x}|\to\infty$}.

For clarity, we adapt the viscosity terms with

(\mu,\lambda)=(\rho,0)

in (
1.4
) and focus on the case when
\varepsilon\in(0,1]

without loss of generality throughout this paper.

Definition 2.6
.

A vector function

(\rho^{\varepsilon},{\mathcal{M}}^{\varepsilon},\Phi^{\varepsilon})
is said to be a weak solution of problem (
1.4
) and (
2.9
)–(
2.10
)
with
(\mu,\lambda)=(\rho,0)

if the following conditions hold:

(i)

\rho^{\varepsilon}(t,\mathbf{x})\geq 0\,\,

a.e.
, and
(\mathcal{M}^{\varepsilon},\frac{\mathcal{M}^{\varepsilon}}{\sqrt{\rho^{\varepsilon}}})(t,\mathbf{x})=\mathbf{0}\,\,

a.e.
on the vacuum states
\{(t,\mathbf{x})\,:\,\rho^{\varepsilon}(t,\mathbf{x})=0\}
,

\displaystyle\rho^{\varepsilon}\in L^{\infty}\big([0,T];L^{\gamma}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\big),\quad\nabla\sqrt{\rho^{\varepsilon}}\in L^{\infty}\big([0,T];L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\big),

\displaystyle\frac{{\mathcal{M}}^{\varepsilon}}{\sqrt{\rho^{\varepsilon}}}\in L^{\infty}\big([0,T];L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\big),\quad\Phi^{\varepsilon}\in L^{\infty}([0,T];L^{\frac{2n}{n-2}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})),

\displaystyle\nabla\Phi^{\varepsilon}\in L^{\infty}([0,T];L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}));

(ii)

For any
t_{2}\geq t_{1}\geq 0
and any
\zeta(t,\mathbf{x})\in C^{1}_{0}(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{n+1})
,
the mass equation
\eqref{1.1}_{1}
holds in the sense:

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}(\rho^{\varepsilon}\zeta)(t_{2},\mathbf{x})\,{\rm d}\mathbf{x}-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}(\rho^{\varepsilon}\zeta)(t_{1},\mathbf{x})\,{\rm d}\mathbf{x}

\displaystyle=\int^{t_{2}}_{t_{1}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\big(\rho^{\varepsilon}\zeta_{t}+{\mathcal{M}}^{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla\zeta\big)(t,\mathbf{x})\,{\rm d}\mathbf{x}{\rm d}t;

(iii)

For any
\boldsymbol{\psi}=(\psi_{1},\cdots,\psi_{n})\in\big(C^{2}_{0}(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{n+1})\big)^{n}
,
the momentum equations
\eqref{1.1}_{2}
hold in the sense:

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}\Big({\mathcal{M}}^{\varepsilon}\cdot\boldsymbol{\psi}_{t}+\frac{{\mathcal{M}}^{\varepsilon}}{\sqrt{\rho^{\varepsilon}}}\cdot\big(\frac{{\mathcal{M}}^{\varepsilon}}{\sqrt{\rho^{\varepsilon}}}\cdot\nabla\big)\boldsymbol{\psi}+p(\rho^{\varepsilon})\,\mbox{\rm div}\,\boldsymbol{\psi}\Big)\,{\rm d}\mathbf{x}{\rm d}t

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}{\mathcal{M}}_{0}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{x})\cdot\boldsymbol{\psi}(0,\mathbf{x})\,{\rm d}\mathbf{x}

\displaystyle=-\varepsilon\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}\Big\{\frac{1}{2}{\mathcal{M}}^{\varepsilon}\cdot\big(\Delta\boldsymbol{\psi}+\nabla\mbox{\rm div}\,\boldsymbol{\psi}\big)+\frac{{\mathcal{M}}^{\varepsilon}}{\sqrt{\rho^{\varepsilon}}}\cdot\big(\nabla\sqrt{\rho^{\varepsilon}}\cdot\nabla\big)\boldsymbol{\psi}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\quad\,\,\,+\nabla\sqrt{\rho^{\varepsilon}}\cdot\big(\frac{{\mathcal{M}}^{\varepsilon}}{\sqrt{\rho^{\varepsilon}}}\cdot\nabla\big)\boldsymbol{\psi}\Big\}\,{\rm d}\mathbf{x}{\rm d}t+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}(\rho^{\varepsilon}\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}\Phi^{\varepsilon}\cdot\boldsymbol{\psi})(t,\mathbf{x})\,{\rm d}\mathbf{x}{\rm d}t;

(iv)

For any
t\geq 0
and
\phi(\mathbf{x})\in C_{0}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
,

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\nabla\Phi^{\varepsilon}(t,\mathbf{x})\cdot\nabla\phi(\mathbf{x})\,{\rm d}\mathbf{x}=-\kappa\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\rho^{\varepsilon}(t,\mathbf{x})\,\phi(\mathbf{x})\,{\rm d}\mathbf{x}.

Consider spherically symmetric solutions of form (
1.5
).
Then systems (
1.1
) and (
1.4
) for such solutions
become (
1.8
) and (
1.9
), respectively.

A pair

(\eta(\rho,m),q(\rho,m))
of functions of
(\rho,m)

is called an entropy pair of the
1
-D Euler system (
i.e.
, consisting of the first two equations of system (
1.8
) with
n=1
and
\Phi\equiv 0
)
if the pair
(\eta(\rho,m),q(\rho,m))
satisfies

\partial_{t}\eta(\rho(t,r),m(t,r))+\partial_{r}q(\rho(t,r),m(t,r))=0

for any smooth solution
(\rho,m)(t,r)
of the 1-D Euler system;
see Lax
[
44
]
.
Furthermore,
\eta(\rho,m)
is called a weak entropy if

\displaystyle\eta|_{\rho=0}=0\qquad\text{for any fixed $u=\frac{m}{\rho}$}.

From
[
53
]
,
it is known (
cf
.
[
8
,
9
,
53
]
)
that any weak entropy
(\eta,q)
can be represented by

(2.11)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\displaystyle\eta^{\psi}(\rho,m)=\eta(\rho,\rho u)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\chi(\rho;s-u)\psi(s)\,{\rm d}s,\\[8.53581pt]
\displaystyle q^{\psi}(\rho,m)=q(\rho,\rho u)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}(\theta s+(1-\theta)u)\chi(\rho;s-u)\psi(s)\,{\rm d}s,\end{cases}

where
\chi(\rho;s-u)=[\rho^{2\theta}-(s-u)^{2}]_{+}^{\mathfrak{b}}
is the kernel
with
\mathfrak{b}=\frac{3-\gamma}{2(\gamma-1)}>-\frac{1}{2}

and
\theta=\frac{\gamma-1}{2}.

In particular, when
\psi(s)=\frac{1}{2}s^{2},

the entropy pair is the pair of the mechanical energy and the associated energy flux:

(2.12)

\displaystyle\eta^{*}(\rho,m)=\frac{m^{2}}{2\rho}+\rho e(\rho),\quad q^{*}(\rho,m)=\frac{m^{3}}{2\rho^{2}}+m(\rho e(\rho))^{\prime}.

Theorem 2.7

(Main Theorem II: Existence and inviscid limit for CNSPEs)

Consider CNSPEs (
1.4
) with
n\geq 3

and the spherically symmetric approximate initial data (
2.9
) satisfying that

(2.13)

\displaystyle(\rho^{\varepsilon}_{0},m_{0}^{\varepsilon})(r)\longrightarrow(\rho_{0},m_{0})(r)\quad\mbox{in $L^{q}([0,\infty);r^{n-1}{\rm d}r)\times L^{1}([0,\infty);r^{n-1}{\rm d}r)$},

(2.14)

\displaystyle(E_{0}^{\varepsilon},E_{1}^{\varepsilon})\longrightarrow(E_{0},0),

as
\varepsilon\to 0+
for
q=\max\{\gamma,\frac{2n}{n+2}\}
, and

(2.15)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\rho^{\varepsilon}_{0}(r)\,r^{n-1}{\rm d}r=\frac{M}{\omega_{n}},\qquad E_{1}^{\varepsilon}\leq C(1+M)\varepsilon,

(2.16)

\displaystyle E_{0}^{\varepsilon}:=\begin{cases}\displaystyle\omega_{n}\int_{0}^{\infty}\Big(\frac{1}{2}\Big|\frac{m_{0}^{\varepsilon}}{\sqrt{\rho_{0}^{\varepsilon}}}\Big|^{2}+\rho_{0}^{\varepsilon}e(\rho_{0}^{\varepsilon})+\frac{1}{2}|\Phi_{0r}^{\varepsilon}|^{2}\Big)(r)\,r^{n-1}{\rm d}r<\infty\,&\mbox{for $\kappa=-1$},\\[9.95845pt]
\displaystyle\omega_{n}\int_{0}^{\infty}\Big(\frac{1}{2}\Big|\frac{m_{0}^{\varepsilon}}{\sqrt{\rho_{0}^{\varepsilon}}}\Big|^{2}+\rho_{0}^{\varepsilon}e(\rho_{0}^{\varepsilon})\Big)(r)\,r^{n-1}{\rm d}r<\infty\,\,&\mbox{for $\kappa=1$},\end{cases}

(2.17)

\displaystyle\hskip-284.52756ptE_{1}^{\varepsilon}:=\omega_{n}\varepsilon^{2}\int_{0}^{\infty}\big|(\sqrt{\rho_{0}^{\varepsilon}})_{r}\big|^{2}\,r^{n-1}{\rm d}r.

In addition,

(a)

when
\kappa=-1

(
plasmas
)
, assume that
\gamma>1
;

(b)

when
\kappa=1

(
gaseous stars
)
, assume that
\gamma>\frac{2(n-1)}{n}
or that there exists
\varepsilon_{0}\in(0,1]
such that, for
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0}]
,

(2.18)

M<M_{\rm c}^{\varepsilon}(\gamma)\qquad\mbox{ when $\gamma\in(\frac{2n}{n+2},\frac{2(n-1)}{n}]$},

where
M_{\rm c}^{\varepsilon}(\gamma)
is the critical mass defined by replacing
E_{0}
in (
2.8
) with
E_{0}^{\varepsilon}
.

Then the following results hold
:

Part I (Existence of global solutions of CNSPEs).

For each fixed
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0}],
there exists a globally-defined spherically symmetric weak solution
:

\displaystyle(\rho^{\varepsilon},{\mathcal{M}}^{\varepsilon},\Phi^{\varepsilon})(t,\mathbf{x})

\displaystyle=(\rho^{\varepsilon}(t,r),m^{\varepsilon}(t,r)\frac{\mathbf{x}}{r},\Phi^{\varepsilon}(t,r))

\displaystyle=(\rho^{\varepsilon}(t,r),\rho^{\varepsilon}(t,r)u^{\varepsilon}(t,r)\frac{\mathbf{x}}{r},\Phi^{\varepsilon}(t,r))

of problem (
1.4
) and (
2.9
)–(
2.10
) for CNSPEs
in the sense of Definition
2.6
, where

\displaystyle u^{\varepsilon}(t,r)=\frac{m^{\varepsilon}(t,r)}{\rho^{\varepsilon}(t,r)}\quad\mbox{a.e. on $\{(t,r)\,:\,\rho^{\varepsilon}(t,r)\neq 0\}$},

\displaystyle u^{\varepsilon}(t,r)=0\quad\mbox{a.e. on $\{(t,r)\,:\,\rho^{\varepsilon}(t,r)=0\}$}.

Moreover,
(\rho^{\varepsilon},m^{\varepsilon},\Phi^{\varepsilon})(t,r)
satisfies the following
:

(i)

For any fixed
T\in(0,\infty)
, the following uniform bounds hold for any
t\in[0,T]
:

(2.19)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\rho^{\varepsilon}(t,r)\,r^{n-1}{\rm d}r=\int_{0}^{\infty}\rho^{\varepsilon}_{0}(r)\,r^{n-1}{\rm d}r=\frac{M}{\omega_{n}},

(2.20)

\displaystyle\Phi^{\varepsilon}_{r}(t,r)=\frac{\kappa}{r^{n-1}}\int_{0}^{r}\rho^{\varepsilon}(t,z)\,z^{n-1}{\rm d}z,

(2.21)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\eta^{*}(\rho^{\varepsilon},m^{\varepsilon})(t,r)\,r^{n-1}{\rm d}r+\varepsilon\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}(\rho^{\varepsilon}|u^{\varepsilon}|^{2})(t,r)\,r^{n-3}{\rm d}r{\rm d}t

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{0}^{\infty}\Big(\int_{0}^{r}\rho^{\varepsilon}(t,z)\,z^{n-1}{\rm d}z\Big)\rho^{\varepsilon}(t,r)\,r{\rm d}r+\|\Phi^{\varepsilon}(t)\|_{L^{\frac{2n}{n-2}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}

\displaystyle\quad+\|\nabla\Phi^{\varepsilon}(t)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\leq C(M,E_{0}),

(2.22)

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{2}\int_{0}^{\infty}\big|\big(\sqrt{\rho^{\varepsilon}(t,r)}\big)_{r}\big|^{2}r^{n-1}{\rm d}r+\varepsilon\int_{0}^{T}\int_{0}^{\infty}\big|\big((\rho^{\varepsilon})^{\frac{\gamma}{2}}\big)_{r}\big|^{2}r^{n-1}{\rm d}r{\rm d}t

\displaystyle\quad\leq C(M,E_{0},T),

(2.23)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\int_{d}^{D}\big(\rho^{\varepsilon}(t,r)|u^{\varepsilon}(t,r)|^{3}+(\rho^{\varepsilon}(t,r))^{\gamma+\theta}\big)\,{\rm d}r{\rm d}t\leq C(d,D,M,E_{0},T),

(2.24)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\int_{d}^{D}(\rho^{\varepsilon}(t,r))^{\gamma+1}\,{\rm d}r{\rm d}t\leq C(d,D,M,E_{0},T),

for any compact subset
[d,D]\Subset(0,\infty)
,
where and whereafter
C(M,E_{0})>0
,
C(M,E_{0},T)>0
,
and
C(d,D,M,E_{0},T)>0
are three universal constants independent of
\varepsilon
, but may depend on
(\gamma,n)

and
(d,D,M,E_{0},T)
, respectively.

(ii)

The following energy inequality holds for both
\kappa=\pm 1
:

(2.25)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\Big(\frac{1}{2}\Big|\frac{{\mathcal{M}}^{\varepsilon}}{\sqrt{\rho^{\varepsilon}}}\Big|^{2}+\rho^{\varepsilon}e(\rho^{\varepsilon})-\frac{\kappa}{2}|\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}\Phi^{\varepsilon}|^{2}\Big)(t,\mathbf{x})\,{\rm d}\mathbf{x}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\Big(\frac{1}{2}\Big|\frac{{\mathcal{M}}_{0}^{\varepsilon}}{\sqrt{\rho_{0}^{\varepsilon}}}\Big|^{2}+\rho_{0}^{\varepsilon}e(\rho_{0}^{\varepsilon})-\frac{\kappa}{2}|\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}\Phi_{0}^{\varepsilon}|^{2}\Big)\,{\rm d}\mathbf{x}\qquad\,\,\mbox{for $t\geq 0$}.

(iii)

Let
(\eta^{\psi},q^{\psi})
be an entropy pair defined in (
2.11
)
for a smooth function
\psi(s)
of compact support on
\mathbb{R}
.
Then, for
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0}]
,

(2.26)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\eta^{\psi}(\rho^{\varepsilon},m^{\varepsilon})+\partial_{r}q^{\psi}(\rho^{\varepsilon},m^{\varepsilon})\qquad\mbox{is compact in }\,H^{-1}_{\rm loc}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}),

where
H^{-1}_{\rm loc}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})
represents
H^{-1}((0,T]\times I)
for any
T>0
and bounded open subset
I\Subset(0,\infty)
,
and
\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}:=\{(t,r)\ :\ t\in(0,\infty),\ r\in(0,\infty)\}
.

Part II (Inviscid limit and existence of global solutions of CEPEs).
For the global weak
solutions
(\rho^{\varepsilon},{\mathcal{M}}^{\varepsilon},\Phi^{\varepsilon})(t,\mathbf{x})=(\rho^{\varepsilon}(t,r),m^{\varepsilon}(t,r)\frac{\mathbf{x}}{r},\Phi^{\varepsilon}(t,r))

of problem (
1.4
) and (
2.9
)–(
2.10
) for CNSPEs
established in Part
I
,
there exist both a subsequence
(
still denoted
)

(\rho^{\varepsilon},m^{\varepsilon},\Phi^{\varepsilon})(t,r)
and a vector function
(\rho,m,\Phi)(t,r)

such that, as
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0
,

\displaystyle(\rho^{\varepsilon},m^{\varepsilon})(t,r)\longrightarrow(\rho,m)(t,r)\qquad\mbox{in $L^{q_{1}}_{\rm loc}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})\times L^{q_{2}}_{\rm loc}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})$},

with
q_{1}\in[1,\gamma+1)
and
q_{2}\in[1,\frac{3(\gamma+1)}{\gamma+3})
, and

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\Phi^{\varepsilon}\rightharpoonup\Phi\quad\mbox{weakly in $L^{2}(0,T;H^{1}_{\rm loc}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))$},\\
&\Phi_{r}^{\varepsilon}(t,r)r^{n-1}=\kappa\int_{0}^{r}\rho^{\varepsilon}(t,z)\,z^{n-1}{\rm d}z\\
&\qquad\qquad\quad\longrightarrow\,\Phi_{r}(t,r)r^{n-1}=\kappa\int_{0}^{r}\rho(t,z)\,z^{n-1}{\rm d}z\quad\mbox{a.e. $(t,r)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}$},\\
&\int_{0}^{\infty}|\Phi_{r}^{\varepsilon}(t,r)-\Phi_{r}(t,r)|^{2}\,r^{n-1}{\rm d}r\to 0\qquad\mbox{if $\gamma>\frac{2n}{n+2}$},\end{split}

with
\displaystyle(\rho,{\mathcal{M}},\Phi)(t,\mathbf{x}):=(\rho(t,r),\ m(t,r)\frac{\mathbf{x}}{r},\ \Phi(t,r))

to be a global spherically symmetric finite-energy
solution of problem (
1.1
)–(
1.3
)
for CEPEs in the sense of Definition
2.1
.

Remark 2.8
.

In Theorem
2.7
, the approximate initial data functions
(\rho_{0}^{\varepsilon},{\mathcal{M}}_{0}^{\varepsilon},\Phi_{0}^{\varepsilon})

satisfying conditions (
2.13
)–(
2.18
) are constructed
in Lemmas
A.2
–
A.10
in Appendix A.
The restriction,
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0}]
,
for the gaseous star case
\kappa=1
with
\gamma\in(\frac{2n}{n+2},\frac{2(n-1)}{n}]

is mainly due to the construction of approximate initial data in Appendix A.
Then Theorem
2.2
is a direct corollary of Theorem
2.7
.
From now on, we always assume
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0}]

without loss of our main objectives for the inviscid limit.

3
Construction and Uniform Estimates of Approximate Solutions

In order to deal with the difficulties for the appearance of cavitation and the singularity at the origin,
besides shock waves,
as well as uniform estimates of approximate solutions,
we construct our approximate solutions via the following approximate free boundary
problem for CNSPEs:

(3.1)

\begin{cases}\displaystyle\rho_{t}+(\rho u)_{r}+\frac{n-1}{r}\rho u=0,\\[5.69054pt]
\displaystyle(\rho u)_{t}+(\rho u^{2}+p(\rho))_{r}+\frac{n-1}{r}\rho u^{2}+\frac{\kappa\rho}{r^{n-1}}\int_{a}^{r}\rho(t,z)z^{n-1}{\rm d}z\\[5.69054pt]
\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\quad=\varepsilon\big(\rho(u_{r}+\frac{n-1}{r}u)\big)_{r}-\varepsilon\frac{n-1}{r}u\rho_{r},\end{cases}

for
(t,r)\in\Omega_{T}
with moving domain:

(3.2)

\Omega_{T}=\{(t,r)\ :\,a\leq r\leq b(t),\,0\leq t\leq T\},

where
\{r=b(t):0<t\leq T\}
is a free boundary determined by

(3.3)

\begin{cases}b^{\prime}(t)=u(t,b(t))\qquad\mbox{for $t>0$},\\
b(0)=b,\end{cases}

and
a=b^{-1}
with
b\gg 1
.
On the free boundary
r=b(t)
, the stress-free boundary condition is chosen:

(3.4)

\big(p(\rho)-\varepsilon\rho(u_{r}+\frac{n-1}{r}u)\big)(t,b(t))=0\qquad\mbox{for $t>0$}.

On the fixed boundary
r=a=b^{-1}
, we impose the Dirichlet boundary condition:

(3.5)

u|_{r=a}=0\qquad\,\mbox{for $t>0$}.

The initial condition is

(3.6)

(\rho,\rho u)|_{t=0}=(\rho_{0}^{\varepsilon,b},\rho_{0}^{\varepsilon,b}u_{0}^{\varepsilon,b})(r)\qquad\,\mbox{for $r\in[a,b]$}.

We always assume that the initial data functions
(\rho_{0}^{\varepsilon,b},u_{0}^{\varepsilon,b})(r)
are smooth
and compatible with the boundary conditions (
3.4
)–(
3.5
),
and
0<C_{\varepsilon,b}^{-1}\leq\rho_{0}^{\varepsilon,b}(r)\leq C_{\varepsilon,b}<\infty
.

For later use, we define

(3.7)

\displaystyle E_{0}^{\varepsilon,b}:=\begin{cases}\displaystyle\int_{a}^{b}\Big\{\rho_{0}^{\varepsilon,b}\big(\frac{1}{2}|u_{0}^{\varepsilon,b}|^{2}+e(\rho_{0}^{\varepsilon,b})\big)+\frac{1}{2r^{2(n-1)}}\Big(\int_{a}^{r}\rho_{0}^{\varepsilon,b}(z)\,z^{n-1}{\rm d}z\Big)^{2}\Big\}\,\omega_{n}r^{n-1}{\rm d}r\\
\hskip 256.0748pt\mbox{for $\kappa=-1$},\\[8.53581pt]
\displaystyle\int_{a}^{b}\rho_{0}^{\varepsilon,b}\big(\frac{1}{2}|u_{0}^{\varepsilon,b}|^{2}+e(\rho_{0}^{\varepsilon,b})\big)\,\omega_{n}r^{n-1}{\rm d}r\hskip 92.75581pt\mbox{for $\kappa=1$},\end{cases}

(3.8)

\displaystyle E_{1}^{\varepsilon,b}:=\varepsilon^{2}\int_{a}^{b}\big|\big(\sqrt{\rho_{0}^{\varepsilon,b}}\big)_{r}\big|^{2}\,\omega_{n}r^{n-1}{\rm d}r.\hskip 142.26378pt

When
\kappa=1
, for the given total energy
E_{0}^{\varepsilon,b}>0
, similar to (
2.8
), we define the critical mass:

(3.9)

\displaystyle M_{\rm c}^{\varepsilon,b}(\gamma):=\begin{cases}B_{n,\gamma}^{-\frac{n}{2}}\qquad\qquad\qquad\quad&\mbox{for $\gamma=\frac{2(n-1)}{n}$},\\[5.69054pt]
\left(\frac{(n-2)B_{n,\gamma}}{n(\gamma-1)}\right)^{-\frac{n(\gamma-1)}{(n+2)\gamma-2n}}\,\left(\frac{(n-2)E_{0}^{\varepsilon,b}}{(2(n-1)-n\gamma)\omega_{n}}\right)^{-\frac{2(n-1)-n\gamma}{(n+2)\gamma-2n}}\,&\mbox{for $\gamma\in(\frac{2n}{n+2},\frac{2(n-1)}{n})$}.\end{cases}

For the initial data
(\rho^{\varepsilon}_{0},m^{\varepsilon}_{0})
imposed in (
2.9
) satisfying (
2.13
)–(
2.18
),
it follows from Lemma
A.10
in Appendix A that
there exists a sequence of smooth functions
(\rho^{\varepsilon,b}_{0},u_{0}^{\varepsilon,b})
defined on
[a,b]
such that,
as
b\rightarrow\infty
,

(3.10)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}(\rho_{0}^{\varepsilon,b},\rho_{0}^{\varepsilon,b}u_{0}^{\varepsilon,b})\longrightarrow(\rho_{0}^{\varepsilon},m_{0}^{\varepsilon})\quad\mbox{in $L^{\hat{q}}([a,b];r^{n-1}{\rm d}r)\times L^{1}([a,b];r^{n-1}{\rm d}r)$},\\[4.2679pt]
(E_{0}^{\varepsilon,b},E_{1}^{\varepsilon,b})\longrightarrow(E_{0}^{\varepsilon},E_{1}^{\varepsilon}),\end{cases}

with
\hat{q}\in\{1,\gamma\}
when
\kappa=1
(gaseous stars) and
\hat{q}\in\{1,\gamma,\frac{2n}{n+2}\}
when
\kappa=-1
(plasmas), and

(3.11)

\displaystyle\int_{a}^{b}\rho_{0}^{\varepsilon,b}(r)\,r^{n-1}{\rm d}r=\frac{M}{\omega_{n}}>0,\qquad E_{1}^{\varepsilon,b}+E_{1}^{\varepsilon}\leq C(1+M)\varepsilon,

(3.12)

\displaystyle\,\,\rho_{0}^{\varepsilon,b}(b)\cong b^{-(n-\alpha)}\qquad\mbox{with $\alpha:=\min\{\frac{1}{2},(1-\frac{1}{\gamma})n\}$}.

Moreover, for each fixed
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0}]
, there exists a large constant
\mathfrak{B}(\varepsilon)>0
such that, when
\kappa=1
with
\gamma\in(\frac{2n}{n+2},\frac{2(n-1)}{n}]
,

(3.13)

M<M_{\rm c}^{\varepsilon,b}(\gamma)\qquad\,\,\mbox{for $b\geq\mathfrak{B}(\varepsilon)$}.

Property (
3.12
) is important for us to close the BD-type entropy estimate in Lemma
3.3
below.

Once problem (
3.1
)–(
3.6
) is solved, we define the potential function
\Phi
to be the solution of the Poisson equation:

(3.14)

\displaystyle\Delta\Phi=\kappa\rho\,\mathbf{1}_{\Omega_{t}},\qquad\lim_{|\textbf{x}|\rightarrow\infty}\Phi=0,

with
\Omega_{t}=\{\textbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\,:\,a\leq|\textbf{x}|\leq b(t)\}
,
for which we have extended
\rho
to be zero outside
\Omega_{t}
, where
\mathbf{1}_{\Omega_{t}}
is the indicator function
of
\Omega_{t}
(which is
1
when
\textbf{x}\in\Omega_{t}
and
0
otherwise).
In fact, we can show that
\Phi(t,\mathbf{x})=\Phi(t,r)
with

(3.15)

\Phi_{r}(t,r)=\begin{cases}\displaystyle 0\qquad&\mbox{for $0\leq r\leq a$},\\[5.69054pt]
\displaystyle\frac{\kappa}{r^{n-1}}\int_{a}^{r}\rho(t,z)\,z^{n-1}{\rm d}z\quad&\mbox{for $a\leq r\leq b(t)$},\\[8.53581pt]
\displaystyle\frac{\kappa M}{\omega_{n}r^{n-1}}\quad&\mbox{for $r\geq b(t)$}.\end{cases}

In this section, parameters
(\varepsilon,b)
are fixed such that
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0}]
and
b\geq\mathfrak{B}(\varepsilon)
.
For
n=3
, the existence of global smooth solutions
(\rho^{\varepsilon,b},u^{\varepsilon,b})
of problem (
3.1
)–(
3.6
)
has been proved by Duan-Li
[
19
]
for
\gamma\in(\frac{6}{5},\frac{4}{3}]
and
\kappa=1
with
0<\rho^{\varepsilon,b}(t,r)<\infty
.
In fact, for
n\geq 3
, the global existence of smooth solutions of our approximate problem (
3.1
)–(
3.6
)
can be obtained by using similar arguments as in
[
19
, §3]
for
\kappa=-1
with
\gamma\in(1,\infty)
,
and for
\kappa=1
with
\gamma\in(\frac{2(n-1)}{n},\infty)
, or with
\gamma\in(\frac{2n}{n+2},\frac{2(n-1)}{n}]

and
M<M_{\rm c}^{\varepsilon,b}(\gamma)
,
so we omit the details here.

Notice that the upper and lower bound of
\rho^{\varepsilon,b}
in
[
19
]
depend on parameters
(\varepsilon,b)
.
Therefore, some careful uniform estimates, independent of
b
, are required so that we can take the limit:
b\rightarrow\infty

to obtain the global weak solutions of problem (
1.4
)
and (
2.9
)–(
2.10
)
in §4 below
as approximate solutions of problem (
1.1
)–(
1.3
).
Throughout this section, for simplicity, we drop the superscript in both the approximate solutions

(\rho^{\varepsilon,b},u^{\varepsilon,b})(t,r)
and the approximate initial data
(\rho^{\varepsilon,b}_{0},u^{\varepsilon,b}_{0})

when no confusion arises.

For strong solutions, it is convenient to deal with IBVP (
3.1
)–(
3.6
) in the Lagrangian coordinates.
It follows from (
3.3
) that

\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}t}\int_{a}^{b(t)}\rho(t,r)\,r^{n-1}{\rm d}r=(\rho u)(t,b(t))b(t)^{n-1}-\int_{a}^{b(t)}(\rho ur^{n-1})_{r}(t,r)\,{\rm d}r=0,

which yields that

(3.16)

\int_{a}^{b(t)}\rho(t,r)\,r^{n-1}{\rm d}r=\int_{a}^{b}\rho_{0}(r)\,r^{n-1}{\rm d}r=\frac{M}{\omega_{n}}\qquad\mbox{for any $t\geq 0$}.

For
r\in[a,b(t)]
and
t\in[0,T]
, we define the Lagrangian coordinates
(\tau,x)
as

x(t,r)=\int_{a}^{r}\rho(t,y)\,y^{n-1}dy,\quad\tau=t,

which translates domain
[0,T]\times[a,b(t)]
into a fixed domain
[0,T]\times[0,\frac{M}{\omega_{n}}]
.
A direct calculation shows that

\displaystyle\nabla_{(t,r)}x=(-\rho ur^{n-1},\rho r^{n-1}),\,\,\,\nabla_{(t,r)}\tau=(1,0),

\displaystyle\nabla_{(\tau,x)}r=(u,\rho^{-1}r^{1-n}),\,\,\,\nabla_{(\tau,x)}t=(1,0).

Applying the Euler-Lagrange transformation, IBVP (
3.1
)–(
3.3
) becomes

(3.17)

\begin{cases}\displaystyle\rho_{\tau}+\rho^{2}(r^{n-1}u)_{x}=0,\\[5.69054pt]
\displaystyle u_{\tau}+r^{n-1}p_{x}=-\kappa\frac{x}{r^{n-1}}+\varepsilon r^{n-1}\big(\rho^{2}(r^{n-1}u)_{x}\big)_{x}-(n-1)\varepsilon r^{n-2}u\rho_{x},\end{cases}

for
(\tau,x)\in[0,T]\times[0,\frac{M}{\omega_{n}}]
, and

(3.18)

u(\tau,0)=0,\quad\big(p-\varepsilon\rho^{2}(r^{n-1}u)_{x}\big)(\tau,\frac{M}{\omega_{n}})=0\qquad\,\mbox{for $\tau\in[0,T]$},

where
r=r(\tau,x)
is defined by

(3.19)

\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}\tau}r(\tau,x)=u(\tau,x)\qquad\mbox{for $x\in[0,\frac{M}{\omega_{n}}]$ and $\tau\in[0,T]$},

and the fixed boundary
x=\frac{M}{\omega_{n}}
corresponds to the free boundary
b(\tau)=r(\tau,\frac{M}{\omega_{n}})

in the Eulerian coordinates.

Lemma 3.1

(Basic energy estimate)

Any smooth solution
(\rho,u)(t,r)
of problem (
3.1
)–(
3.6
) satisfies the following energy identity
:

(3.20)

\displaystyle\int_{a}^{b(t)}\big(\frac{1}{2}\rho u^{2}+\rho e(\rho)\big)(t,r)\,r^{n-1}{\rm d}r-\frac{\kappa}{2}\int_{a}^{\infty}\frac{1}{r^{n-1}}\Big(\int_{a}^{r}\rho(t,z)\,z^{n-1}{\rm d}z\Big)^{2}{\rm d}r

\displaystyle\quad+\varepsilon\int_{0}^{t}\int_{a}^{b(s)}\big(\rho u_{r}^{2}+(n-1)\rho\frac{u^{2}}{r^{2}}\big)(s,r)\,r^{n-1}{\rm d}r{\rm d}s

\displaystyle\quad+(n-1)\varepsilon\int_{0}^{t}(\rho u^{2})(s,b(s))b(s)^{n-2}\,{\rm d}s

\displaystyle=\int_{a}^{b}\big(\frac{1}{2}\rho_{0}u_{0}^{2}+\rho_{0}e(\rho_{0})\big)(r)\,r^{n-1}{\rm d}r-\frac{\kappa}{2}\int_{a}^{\infty}\frac{1}{r^{n-1}}\Big(\int_{a}^{r}\rho_{0}(z)\,z^{n-1}{\rm d}z\Big)^{2}\,{\rm d}r,

where
\rho(t,r)
is understood to be
0
for
r\in[0,a]\cup(b(t),\infty)
in the second term of
the right-hand side
(
RHS
)
and the second term of the left-hand side
(
LHS
)
. In particular, the following
estimates hold
:

Case
1:
If
\kappa=-1

(
plasmas
)
with
\gamma>1
, then

(3.21)

\displaystyle\int_{a}^{b(t)}\big(\frac{1}{2}\rho u^{2}+\rho e(\rho)\big)(t,r)\,r^{n-1}{\rm d}r+\frac{1}{2}\int_{a}^{\infty}\frac{1}{r^{n-1}}\Big(\int_{a}^{r}\rho(t,z)\,z^{n-1}{\rm d}z\Big)^{2}{\rm d}r

\displaystyle\quad+\varepsilon\int_{0}^{t}\int_{a}^{b(s)}\big(\rho u_{r}^{2}+(n-1)\rho\frac{u^{2}}{r^{2}}\big)(s,r)\,r^{n-1}{\rm d}r{\rm d}s

\displaystyle\quad+(n-1)\varepsilon\int_{0}^{t}(\rho u^{2})(s,b(s))b(s)^{n-2}\,{\rm d}s

\displaystyle=\int_{a}^{b}\Big(\big(\frac{1}{2}\rho_{0}u_{0}^{2}+\rho_{0}e(\rho_{0})\big)(r)+\frac{1}{2r^{2(n-1)}}\big(\int_{a}^{r}\rho_{0}(z)\,z^{n-1}{\rm d}z\big)^{2}\Big)\,r^{n-1}{\rm d}r:=\frac{E_{0}^{\varepsilon,b}}{\omega_{n}}.

Case
2:

If
\kappa=1

(
gaseous stars
)
with
\gamma\in(\frac{2n}{n+2},\frac{2(n-1)}{n}]
and
M<M_{\rm c}^{\varepsilon,b}(\gamma)
,
then

(3.22)

\displaystyle\int_{a}^{b(t)}\big(\frac{1}{2}\rho u^{2}+C_{\gamma}\rho e(\rho)\big)(t,r)\,r^{n-1}{\rm d}r

\displaystyle\quad+\varepsilon\int_{0}^{t}\int_{a}^{b(s)}\big(\rho u_{r}^{2}+(n-1)\rho\frac{u^{2}}{r^{2}}\big)(s,r)\,r^{n-1}{\rm d}r{\rm d}s

\displaystyle\quad+(n-1)\varepsilon\int_{0}^{t}(\rho u^{2})(s,b(s))b(s)^{n-2}\,{\rm d}s

\displaystyle\leq\int_{a}^{b}\big(\frac{1}{2}\rho_{0}u_{0}^{2}+\rho_{0}e(\rho_{0})\big)(r)\,r^{n-1}{\rm d}r=:\frac{E_{0}^{\varepsilon,b}}{\omega_{n}},

where the positive constant
C_{\gamma}>0
is defined as

(3.23)

\displaystyle C_{\gamma}:=\begin{cases}\displaystyle 1-B_{n,\gamma}M^{\frac{2}{n}}\qquad&\mbox{for $\gamma=\frac{2(n-1)}{n}$},\\[8.53581pt]
\displaystyle\frac{2(n-1)-n\gamma}{n-2}\qquad&\mbox{for $\gamma\in(\frac{2n}{n+2},\frac{2(n-1)}{n})$}.\end{cases}

Case
3:
If
\kappa=1

(
gaseous stars
)
with
\gamma>\frac{2(n-1)}{n}
, then

(3.24)

\displaystyle\int_{a}^{b(t)}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\big(\rho u^{2}+\rho e(\rho)\big)(t,r)\,r^{n-1}{\rm d}r

\displaystyle+\varepsilon\int_{0}^{t}\int_{a}^{b(s)}\big(\rho u_{r}^{2}+(n-1)\rho\frac{u^{2}}{r^{2}}\big)(s,r)\,r^{n-1}{\rm d}r{\rm d}s

\displaystyle+(n-1)\varepsilon\int_{0}^{t}(\rho u^{2})(s,b(s))b(s)^{n-2}\,{\rm d}s

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\,\,\frac{E_{0}^{\varepsilon,b}}{\omega_{n}}+C(M),

where
C(M)>0
is some positive constant depending only on the total initial-mass
M
.

Proof.

We divide the proof into four steps.

1. Multiplying
\eqref{2.10}_{2}
by
u
and then integrating the resultant equation over
x\in[0,\frac{M}{\omega_{n}}]
yield that

(3.25)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}\tau}\int_{0}^{\frac{M}{\omega_{n}}}u^{2}{\rm d}x+\int_{0}^{\frac{M}{\omega_{n}}}\big(p-\varepsilon\rho^{2}(r^{n-1}u)_{x}\big)_{x}u\,r^{n-1}{\rm d}x

\displaystyle=-\varepsilon(n-1)\int_{0}^{\frac{M}{\omega_{n}}}u^{2}\rho_{x}r^{n-2}\,{\rm d}x-\kappa\int_{0}^{\frac{M}{\omega_{n}}}\frac{x}{r^{n-1}}u\,{\rm d}x.

For the second term of (
3.25
)-LHS (
i.e.
, the left-hand side of (
3.25
)),
it follows from
\eqref{2.10}_{1}
and (
3.18
)–(
3.19
) and integration by parts that

(3.26)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\frac{M}{\omega_{n}}}\big(p(\rho)-\varepsilon\rho^{2}(r^{n-1}u)_{x}\big)_{x}u\,r^{n-1}{\rm d}x

\displaystyle=-\int_{0}^{\frac{M}{\omega_{n}}}\big(p(\rho)-\varepsilon\rho^{2}(r^{n-1}u)_{x}\big)(r^{n-1}u)_{x}\,{\rm d}x

\displaystyle=-\int_{0}^{\frac{M}{\omega_{n}}}p(\rho)(r^{n-1}u)_{x}\,{\rm d}x+\varepsilon\int_{0}^{\frac{M}{\omega_{n}}}\rho^{2}\big((r^{n-1}u)_{x}\big)^{2}\,{\rm d}x

\displaystyle=a_{0}\int_{0}^{\frac{M}{\omega_{n}}}\rho^{\gamma-2}\rho_{\tau}\,{\rm d}x+\varepsilon\int_{0}^{\frac{M}{\omega_{n}}}\rho^{2}\big(r^{n-1}u_{x}+(n-1)r^{n-2}r_{x}u\big)^{2}\,{\rm d}x

\displaystyle=\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}\tau}\int_{0}^{\frac{M}{\omega_{n}}}e(\rho)\,{\rm d}x

\displaystyle\quad+\varepsilon\int_{0}^{\frac{M}{\omega_{n}}}\Big(\rho^{2}(r^{n-1}u_{x})^{2}+(n-1)^{2}\frac{u^{2}}{r^{2}}+2(n-1)r^{n-2}\rho uu_{x}\Big){\rm d}x.

For the first term of (
3.25
)-RHS (
i.e.
, the right-hand side of (
3.25
)),
a direct calculation shows that

(3.27)

\displaystyle(n-1)\varepsilon\int_{0}^{\frac{M}{\omega_{n}}}\rho_{x}u^{2}r^{n-2}{\rm d}x

\displaystyle=-(n-1)\varepsilon\int_{0}^{\frac{M}{\omega_{n}}}\big(2r^{n-2}\rho uu_{x}+(n-2)\frac{u^{2}}{r^{2}}\big)\,{\rm d}x

\displaystyle\quad\,+(n-1)\varepsilon(\rho u^{2}r^{n-2})(\tau,\frac{M}{\omega_{n}}).

For the last term of (
3.25
)-RHS, it follows from (
3.19
) that

(3.28)

\displaystyle-\kappa\int_{0}^{\frac{M}{\omega_{n}}}\frac{x}{r^{n-1}}u\,{\rm d}x=-\kappa\int_{0}^{\frac{M}{\omega_{n}}}\frac{x}{r^{n-1}}r_{\tau}\,{\rm d}x=\frac{\kappa}{n-2}\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}\tau}\int_{0}^{\frac{M}{\omega_{n}}}\frac{x}{r^{n-2}}\,{\rm d}x.

Substituting (
3.26
)–(
3.28
) into (
3.25
), we have

(3.29)

\displaystyle\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}\tau}\Big\{\int_{0}^{\frac{M}{\omega_{n}}}\big(\frac{1}{2}u^{2}+e(\rho)\big)\,{\rm d}x-\frac{\kappa}{n-2}\int_{0}^{\frac{M}{\omega_{n}}}\frac{x}{r^{n-2}}\,{\rm d}x\Big\}

\displaystyle\quad+\varepsilon\int_{0}^{\frac{M}{\omega_{n}}}\big(\rho^{2}(r^{n-1}u_{x})^{2}+(n-1)\frac{u^{2}}{r^{2}}\big)\,{\rm d}x+(n-1)\varepsilon(\rho u^{2}r^{n-2})(\tau,\frac{M}{\omega_{n}})

\displaystyle=0.

Plugging (
3.29
) back to the Eulerian coordinates, we obtain

(3.30)

\displaystyle\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}t}\Big\{\int_{a}^{b(t)}\big(\frac{1}{2}\rho u^{2}+\rho e(\rho)\big)\,r^{n-1}{\rm d}r-\frac{\kappa}{n-2}\int_{a}^{b(t)}\big(\int_{a}^{r}\rho\,z^{n-1}{\rm d}z\big)\rho r{\rm d}r\Big\}

\displaystyle\quad+\varepsilon\int_{a}^{b(t)}\big(\rho u_{r}^{2}+(n-1)\rho\frac{u^{2}}{r^{2}}\big)\,r^{n-1}{\rm d}r+(n-1)\varepsilon(\rho u^{2})(t,b(t))b(t)^{n-2}

\displaystyle=0.

Then integrating (
3.30
) over
[0,t]
leads to

(3.31)

\displaystyle\int_{a}^{b(t)}\big(\frac{1}{2}\rho u^{2}+\rho e(\rho)\big)\,r^{n-1}{\rm d}r-\frac{\kappa}{n-2}\int_{a}^{b(t)}\big(\int_{a}^{r}\rho(t,z)\,z^{n-1}{\rm d}z\big)\rho\,r{\rm d}r

\displaystyle\quad+\varepsilon\int_{0}^{t}\int_{a}^{b(s)}\big(\rho u_{r}^{2}+(n-1)\rho\frac{u^{2}}{r^{2}}\big)\,r^{n-1}{\rm d}r{\rm d}s

\displaystyle\quad+(n-1)\varepsilon\int_{0}^{t}(\rho u^{2})(s,b(s))b(s)^{n-2}\,{\rm d}s

\displaystyle=\int_{a}^{b}\big(\frac{1}{2}\rho_{0}u_{0}^{2}+\rho_{0}e(\rho_{0})\big)\,r^{n-1}{\rm d}r-\frac{\kappa}{n-2}\int_{a}^{b}\big(\int_{a}^{r}\rho_{0}(z)\,z^{n-1}{\rm d}z\big)\rho_{0}(r)\,r{\rm d}r.

2. To close the estimates, we need to control the terms involving potential
\Phi
.
Noting (
3.15
), a direct calculation shows that

(3.32)

\displaystyle\frac{\kappa}{n-2}\int_{a}^{b(t)}\big(\int_{a}^{r}\rho\,z^{n-1}{\rm d}z\big)\rho\,r{\rm d}r

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(n-2)\kappa}\int_{a}^{b(t)}(r^{n-1}\Phi_{r})_{r}\Phi_{r}\,r{\rm d}r

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\kappa}\Big\{\int_{a}^{b(t)}|\Phi_{r}|^{2}\,r^{n-1}{\rm d}r+\frac{1}{(n-2)b(t)^{n-2}}\big(\int_{a}^{b(t)}\rho\,z^{n-1}{\rm d}z\big)^{2}\Big\}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\kappa}\Big\{\int_{a}^{b(t)}|\Phi_{r}|^{2}\,r^{n-1}{\rm d}r+\left(\frac{M}{\omega_{n}}\right)^{2}\frac{1}{(n-2)b(t)^{n-2}}\Big\}.

On the other hand, it follows from (
3.15
) that

\displaystyle\|\nabla\Phi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}^{2}

\displaystyle=\omega_{n}\Big\{\int_{a}^{b(t)}|\Phi_{r}|^{2}\,r^{n-1}{\rm d}r+\int_{b(t)}^{\infty}|\Phi_{r}|^{2}\,r^{n-1}{\rm d}r\Big\}

\displaystyle=\omega_{n}\Big\{\int_{a}^{b(t)}|\Phi_{r}|^{2}\,r^{n-1}{\rm d}r+\left(\frac{M}{\omega_{n}}\right)^{2}\int_{b(t)}^{\infty}r^{-n+1}{\rm d}r\Big\}

\displaystyle=\omega_{n}\Big\{\int_{a}^{b(t)}|\Phi_{r}|^{2}\,r^{n-1}{\rm d}r+\left(\frac{M}{\omega_{n}}\right)^{2}\frac{1}{(n-2)b(t)^{n-2}}\Big\},

which, together with (
3.32
), yields that

(3.33)

\displaystyle\frac{\kappa}{n-2}\int_{a}^{b(t)}\big(\int_{a}^{r}\rho z^{n-1}{\rm d}z\big)\rho\,r{\rm d}r

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\kappa\omega_{n}}\|\nabla\Phi\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\kappa}\int_{a}^{\infty}\frac{1}{r^{n-1}}\big(\int_{a}^{r}\rho\,z^{n-1}{\rm d}z\big)^{2}{\rm d}r,

where we need to understand
\rho
to be zero for
r\in[0,a)\cup(b(t),\infty)
in the last equality of (
3.33
).

3. Substituting (
3.33
) into (
3.31
), we conclude (
3.20
).
When
\kappa=-1
(plasmas), (
3.21
) follows directly from (
3.20
).

4. When
\kappa=1
(gaseous stars), from (
3.31
) and (
3.33
),
the gravitational potential part has to be carefully controlled.
Multiplying (
3.14
) by
\Phi
and integrating by parts yield

(3.34)

\displaystyle\|\nabla\Phi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\|\Phi\|_{L^{\frac{2n}{n-2}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\,\|\rho\|_{L^{\frac{2n}{n+2}}(\Omega_{t})}\leq\sqrt{A_{n}}\|\nabla\Phi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\,\|\rho\|_{L^{\frac{2n}{n+2}}(\Omega_{t})},

where
the positive constant
A_{n}=\frac{4}{n(n-2)}\omega_{n+1}^{-\frac{2}{n}}>0
is the sharp constant for the Sobolev inequality which is given in Lemma
A.1
.
Then it follows directly from (
3.34
) that

(3.35)

\displaystyle\|\nabla\Phi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq A_{n}\|\rho\|_{L^{\frac{2n}{n+2}}(\Omega_{t})}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq A_{n}\|\rho\|_{L^{1}(\Omega_{t})}^{2(1-\vartheta)}\,\|\rho\|_{L^{\gamma}(\Omega_{t})}^{2\vartheta}

\displaystyle\leq A_{n}\omega_{n}^{\frac{n-2}{n(\gamma-1)}}M^{\frac{(n+2)\gamma-2n}{n(\gamma-1)}}\Big(\int_{a}^{b(t)}\rho^{\gamma}\,r^{n-1}{\rm d}r\Big)^{\frac{n-2}{n(\gamma-1)}},

where
\vartheta=\frac{(n-2)\gamma}{2n(\gamma-1)}
,
and we have used the condition that
\gamma\geq\frac{2n}{n+2}
.

Substituting (
3.35
) into (
3.33
) and using (
A.2
), we have

(3.36)

\displaystyle\frac{\kappa}{n-2}\int_{a}^{b(t)}\big(\int_{a}^{r}\rho\,z^{n-1}{\rm d}z\big)\rho\,r{\rm d}r

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2\kappa}{n(n-2)}\omega_{n+1}^{-\frac{2}{n}}\omega_{n}^{\frac{2(n-1)-n\gamma}{n(\gamma-1)}}\Big(\frac{\gamma-1}{a_{0}}\Big)^{\frac{n-2}{n(\gamma-1)}}M^{\frac{(n+2)\gamma-2n}{n(\gamma-1)}}\,\Big(\int_{a}^{b(t)}\rho e(\rho)\,r^{n-1}{\rm d}r\Big)^{\frac{n-2}{n(\gamma-1)}}

\displaystyle:=B_{n,\gamma}M^{\frac{(n+2)\gamma-2n}{n(\gamma-1)}}\Big(\int_{a}^{b(t)}\rho e(\rho)\,r^{n-1}{\rm d}r\Big)^{\frac{n-2}{n(\gamma-1)}},

where
B_{n,\gamma}
is the constant defined in (
2.8
).

Noting (
3.36
), we use the internal energy to control the gravitational part.
It follows from (
3.36
) that

(3.37)

\displaystyle\int_{a}^{b(t)}\rho e(\rho)\,r^{n-1}{\rm d}r-\frac{\kappa}{n-2}\int_{a}^{b(t)}\Big(\int_{a}^{r}\rho\,z^{n-1}{\rm d}z\Big)\rho\,r{\rm d}r

\displaystyle\geq\int_{a}^{b(t)}\rho e(\rho)\,r^{n-1}{\rm d}r-B_{n,\gamma}M^{\frac{(n+2)\gamma-2n}{n(\gamma-1)}}\Big(\int_{a}^{b(t)}\rho e(\rho)\,r^{n-1}{\rm d}r\Big)^{\frac{n-2}{n(\gamma-1)}}.

For the case that
\kappa=1
with
\gamma>\frac{2(n-1)}{n}
, which implies that
\frac{n-2}{n(\gamma-1)}<1
.
Then it follows from (
3.37
) and the Hölder inequality that

\displaystyle\int_{a}^{b(t)}\rho e(\rho)\,r^{n-1}{\rm d}r-\frac{\kappa}{n-2}\int_{a}^{b(t)}\Big(\int_{a}^{r}\rho\,z^{n-1}{\rm d}z\Big)\rho\,r{\rm d}r

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{2}\int_{a}^{b(t)}\rho e(\rho)\,r^{n-1}{\rm d}r-C(n,M),

which, together with (
3.31
), yields (
3.24
).

For the case that
\kappa=1
with
\gamma=\frac{2(n-1)}{n}
,
i.e.
,
\frac{n-2}{n(\gamma-1)}=1
, we use (
3.31
) and (
3.37
) to obtain

(3.38)

\displaystyle\int_{a}^{b(t)}\rho e(\rho)\,r^{n-1}{\rm d}r-\frac{\kappa}{n-2}\int_{a}^{b(t)}\Big(\int_{a}^{r}\rho\,z^{n-1}{\rm d}z\Big)\rho\,r{\rm d}r

\displaystyle\geq\Big(1-B_{n,\gamma}M^{\frac{2}{n}}\Big)\int_{a}^{b(t)}\rho e(\rho)\,r^{n-1}{\rm d}r:=C_{\gamma}\int_{a}^{b(t)}\rho e(\rho)\,r^{n-1}{\rm d}r,

provided that

M<M_{\rm c}^{\varepsilon,b}(\gamma):=B_{n,\gamma}^{-\frac{n}{2}}
.

For the case that
\kappa=1
with
\gamma\in(\frac{2n}{n+2},\frac{2(n-1)}{n})
, we define

F(s)=s-B_{n,\gamma}M^{\frac{(n+2)\gamma-2n}{n(\gamma-1)}}\,s^{\frac{n-2}{n(\gamma-1)}}\qquad\mbox{for $s\geq 0$}.

A direct calculation shows that

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\displaystyle F^{\prime}(s)=1-\frac{n-2}{n(\gamma-1)}B_{n,\gamma}M^{\frac{(n+2)\gamma-2n}{n(\gamma-1)}}\,s^{\frac{2(n-1)-n\gamma}{n(\gamma-1)}},\\[11.38109pt]
\displaystyle F^{\prime\prime}(s)=-\frac{(2(n-1)-n\gamma)(n-2)}{n^{2}(\gamma-1)^{2}}\,B_{n,\gamma}M^{\frac{(n+2)\gamma-2n}{n(\gamma-1)}}\,s^{\frac{3n-2-2n\gamma}{n(\gamma-1)}},\end{cases}

which yields that
F^{\prime\prime}(s)<0
for any
s>0
if
\gamma<\frac{2(n-1)}{n}
, so that
F(s)
is concave for
s>0
.
We denote

(3.39)

\displaystyle s_{\ast}=\Big(\frac{(n-2)B_{n,\gamma}}{n(\gamma-1)}\Big)^{-\frac{n(\gamma-1)}{2(n-1)-n\gamma}}\,M^{-\frac{(n+2)\gamma-2n}{2(n-1)-n\gamma}},

which is the critical point of
F(s)
so that
F^{\prime}(s_{\ast})=0
.
The maximum of
F(s)
for
s\geq 0
is

\displaystyle F(s_{\ast})=\frac{2(n-1)-n\gamma}{n-2}\Big(\frac{(n-2)B_{n,\gamma}}{n(\gamma-1)}\Big)^{-\frac{n(\gamma-1)}{2(n-1)-n\gamma}}\,M^{-\frac{(n+2)\gamma-2n}{2(n-1)-n\gamma}}>0\qquad\mbox{for $\gamma<\frac{2(n-1)}{n}$}.

Now we claim that, under condition (
3.13
),

(3.40)

\int_{a}^{b(t)}\rho e(\rho)\,r^{n-1}{\rm d}r<s_{\ast}.

Noting that
\frac{2(n-1)-n\gamma}{(n+2)\gamma-2n}>0
for
\gamma\in(\frac{2n}{n+2},\frac{2(n-1)}{n})
,
it follows from (
3.9
), (
3.13
), and (
3.39
) that

(3.41)

F(s_{\ast})>\frac{E_{0}^{\varepsilon,b}}{\omega_{n}},

and

(3.42)

\displaystyle s_{\ast}

\displaystyle>\Big(\frac{(n-2)B_{n,\gamma}}{n(\gamma-1)}\Big)^{-\frac{n(\gamma-1)}{2(n-1)-n\gamma}}\,M_{c}^{\varepsilon,b}(\gamma)^{-\frac{(n+2)\gamma-2n}{2(n-1)-n\gamma}}

\displaystyle=\frac{n-2}{2(n-1)-n\gamma}\frac{E_{0}^{\varepsilon,b}}{\omega_{n}}>\frac{2E_{0}^{\varepsilon,b}}{\omega_{n}},

where we have used
\frac{n-2}{2(n-1)-n\gamma}>1+\frac{n}{2}>2
for
\gamma\in(\frac{2n}{n+2},\frac{2(n-1)}{n})
.
Then it follows from (
3.31
), (
3.37
), and (
3.41
)–(
3.42
) that

(3.43)

\displaystyle F(\int_{a}^{b(t)}\rho e(\rho)\,r^{n-1}{\rm d}r)\leq\frac{E_{0}^{\varepsilon,b}}{\omega_{n}}<F(s_{\ast}),

(3.44)

\displaystyle\int_{a}^{b}\rho_{0}e(\rho_{0})\,r^{n-1}{\rm d}r\leq\frac{E_{0}^{\varepsilon,b}}{\omega_{n}}<s_{\ast}.

Thus, due to the continuity of
\int_{a}^{b(t)}(\rho e(\rho))(t,r)\,r^{n-1}{\rm d}r
with respect to
t
,
(
3.40
) must hold.
Otherwise,
there exists some time
t_{0}>0
such that
\int_{a}^{b(t)}\rho e(\rho)\,r^{n-1}{\rm d}r=s_{\ast}
, which yields

\displaystyle F(\int_{a}^{b(t_{0})}\rho e(\rho)\,r^{n-1}{\rm d}r)=F(s_{\ast})>\frac{E_{0}^{\varepsilon,b}}{\omega_{n}},

which contradicts (
3.43
). Therefore, (
3.40
) always holds under condition (
3.13
).

Now, under condition (
3.13
), it follows from (
3.40
) that

(3.45)

\displaystyle F(\int_{a}^{b(t)}\rho e(\rho)\,r^{n-1}{\rm d}r)

\displaystyle\geq\Big(1-B_{n,\gamma}M^{\frac{(n+2)\gamma-2n}{n(\gamma-1)}}\,s_{\ast}^{\frac{2(n-1)-n\gamma}{n(\gamma-1)}}\Big)\int_{a}^{b(t)}\rho e(\rho)\,r^{n-1}{\rm d}r

\displaystyle=\frac{2(n-1)-n\gamma}{n-2}\int_{a}^{b(t)}\rho e(\rho)\,r^{n-1}{\rm d}r.

Thus, (
3.22
) follows directly from (
3.38
) and (
3.45
).
This completes the proof.
∎

Using (
3.15
), (
3.21
)–(
3.22
), (
3.24
), (
3.33
), and (
A.1
),
we have the following estimates for the potential function
\Phi
.

Corollary 3.2

Under the conditions of Lemma
3.1
,

\displaystyle|r^{n-1}\Phi_{r}(t,r)|\leq\frac{M}{\omega_{n}}\qquad\mbox{for $\,(t,r)\in[0,\infty)\times[0,\infty)$},

\displaystyle\int_{a}^{b(t)}\Big(\int_{a}^{r}\rho(t,z)\,z^{n-1}{\rm d}z\Big)\rho(t,r)\,r{\rm d}r+\|\Phi(t)\|_{L^{\frac{2n}{n-2}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}+\|\nabla\Phi(t)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}

\displaystyle\quad\leq C(M,E_{0})\quad\mbox{for $t\geq 0$}.

For later use, we analyze the behavior of density
\rho
on the free boundary. It follows from
\eqref{2.10}_{1}
and (
3.18
) that

(3.46)

\displaystyle\rho_{\tau}(\tau,\frac{M}{\omega_{n}})=-\frac{a_{0}}{\varepsilon}\rho^{\gamma}(\tau,\frac{M}{\omega_{n}}).

Then we obtain

\displaystyle\rho(\tau,\frac{M}{\omega_{n}})=\rho_{0}(\frac{M}{\omega_{n}})\,\Big(1+\frac{(\gamma-1)^{2}}{\varepsilon}e(\rho_{0}(\frac{M}{\omega_{n}}))\tau\Big)^{-\frac{1}{\gamma-1}}.

In the Eulerian coordinates, it is equivalent to the form:

(3.47)

\displaystyle\rho(t,b(t))=\rho_{0}(b)\Big(1+\frac{(\gamma-1)^{2}}{\varepsilon}e(\rho_{0}(b))t\Big)^{-\frac{1}{\gamma-1}}\leq\rho_{0}(b).

The density behavior on the free boundary (
3.47
) is important, which will be used frequently later.

Lemma 3.3

(BD-type entropy estimate)

Under the conditions of Lemma
3.1
, for any given
T>0
, the following holds for any
t\in[0,T]
:

(3.48)

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{2}\int_{a}^{b(t)}\big|\big(\sqrt{\rho(t,r)}\big)_{r}\big|^{2}\,r^{n-1}{\rm d}r+\frac{3a_{0}\varepsilon}{\gamma}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{a}^{b(s)}\big|\big(\rho^{\frac{\gamma}{2}}\big)_{r}\big|^{2}\,r^{n-1}{\rm d}r{\rm d}s

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{n}p(\rho(t,b(t)))b(t)^{n}+\frac{1}{n\varepsilon}\int_{0}^{t}p(\rho(s,b(s)))p^{\prime}(\rho(s,b(s)))b(s)^{n}\,{\rm d}s

\displaystyle\leq C(E_{0},M,b^{n}\rho_{0}^{\gamma}(b),T)\leq C(E_{0},M,T).

Proof.

We divide the proof into four steps.

1. For convenience, we start with the solution in the Lagrangian coordinates
(\tau,x)
.
It follows from
\eqref{2.10}_{1}
that

\displaystyle\rho_{x\tau}=-\big(\rho^{2}(r^{n-1}u)_{x}\big)_{x},

which, together with
\eqref{2.10}_{2}
, yields that

(3.49)

\displaystyle u_{\tau}+r^{n-1}p_{x}=-\varepsilon r^{n-1}\rho_{x\tau}-\varepsilon(n-1)r^{n-2}u\rho_{x}-\kappa\frac{x}{r^{n-1}}.

Then (
3.49
) can be rewritten by using (
3.19
) as

(3.50)

\displaystyle(u+\varepsilon r^{n-1}\rho_{x})_{\tau}+r^{n-1}p_{x}=-\kappa\frac{x}{r^{n-1}}.

Multiplying (
3.50
) by
u+\varepsilon r^{n-1}\rho_{x}
yields

(3.51)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}\tau}\int_{0}^{\frac{M}{\omega_{n}}}(u+\varepsilon r^{n-1}\rho_{x})^{2}\,{\rm d}x+\varepsilon\int_{0}^{\frac{M}{\omega_{n}}}p(\rho)_{x}\rho_{x}\,r^{2n-2}{\rm d}x

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{0}^{\frac{M}{\omega_{n}}}p(\rho)_{x}u\,r^{n-1}{\rm d}x

\displaystyle=\frac{\kappa}{n-2}\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}\tau}\int_{0}^{\frac{M}{\omega_{n}}}\frac{x}{r^{n-2}}\,{\rm d}x-\kappa\varepsilon\int_{0}^{\frac{M}{\omega_{n}}}\rho_{x}x\,{\rm d}x,

where we have used (
3.28
).
Using
\eqref{2.10}_{1}
, (
3.19
), and (
3.46
), we have

(3.52)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\frac{M}{\omega_{n}}}p(\rho)_{x}u\,r^{n-1}{\rm d}x

\displaystyle=-\int_{0}^{\frac{M}{\omega_{n}}}p(\rho)(r^{n-1}u)_{x}\,{\rm d}x+(p(\rho)ur^{n-1})(\tau,\frac{M}{\omega_{n}})

\displaystyle=\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}\tau}\int_{0}^{\frac{M}{\omega_{n}}}e(\rho)\,{\rm d}x+(p(\rho)r^{n-1})(\tau,\frac{M}{\omega_{n}})\,r_{\tau}(\tau,\frac{M}{\omega_{n}})

\displaystyle=\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}\tau}\int_{0}^{\frac{M}{\omega_{n}}}e(\rho)\,{\rm d}x+\Big(\frac{1}{n}p(\rho(\tau,\frac{M}{\omega_{n}}))b(\tau)^{n}\Big)_{\tau}

\displaystyle\quad\,-\frac{1}{n}p^{\prime}(\rho(\tau,\frac{M}{\omega_{n}}))\rho_{\tau}(\tau,\frac{M}{\omega_{n}})b(\tau)^{n}

\displaystyle=\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}\tau}\int_{0}^{\frac{M}{\omega_{n}}}e(\rho)\,{\rm d}x+\Big(\frac{1}{n}p(\rho(\tau,\frac{M}{\omega_{n}}))b(\tau)^{n}\Big)_{\tau}

\displaystyle\quad\,+\frac{1}{n\varepsilon}p(\rho(\tau,\frac{M}{\omega_{n}}))p^{\prime}(\rho(\tau,\frac{M}{\omega_{n}}))b(\tau)^{n},

(3.53)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\frac{M}{\omega_{n}}}x\rho_{x}\,{\rm d}x=-\int_{0}^{\frac{M}{\omega_{n}}}\rho\,{\rm d}x+\frac{M}{\omega_{n}}\rho(\tau,\frac{M}{\omega_{n}}).

Substituting (
3.52
)–(
3.53
) into (
3.51
) yields

(3.54)

\displaystyle\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}\tau}\Big\{\int_{0}^{\frac{M}{\omega_{n}}}\frac{1}{2}(u+\varepsilon r^{n-1}\rho_{x})^{2}\,{\rm d}x+\int_{0}^{\frac{M}{\omega_{n}}}e(\rho)\,{\rm d}x-\frac{\kappa}{n-2}\int_{0}^{\frac{M}{\omega_{n}}}\frac{x}{r^{n-2}}\,{\rm d}x\Big\}

\displaystyle\quad\,+\varepsilon\int_{0}^{\frac{M}{\omega_{n}}}p^{\prime}(\rho)\rho_{x}^{2}\,r^{2n-2}{\rm d}x+\Big(\frac{1}{n}p(\rho(\tau,\frac{M}{\omega_{n}}))b(\tau)^{n}\Big)_{\tau}

\displaystyle\quad\,+\frac{1}{n\varepsilon}p(\rho(\tau,\frac{M}{\omega_{n}}))p^{\prime}(\rho(\tau,\frac{M}{\omega_{n}}))b(\tau)^{n}

\displaystyle=\varepsilon\kappa\int_{0}^{\frac{M}{\omega_{n}}}\rho\,{\rm d}x-\varepsilon\kappa\frac{M}{\omega_{n}}\rho(\tau,\frac{M}{\omega_{n}}).

Integrating (
3.54
) over
[0,\tau]
, we have

(3.55)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\frac{M}{\omega_{n}}}\frac{1}{2}(u+\varepsilon r^{n-1}\rho_{x})^{2}\,{\rm d}x+\int_{0}^{\frac{M}{\omega_{n}}}e(\rho)\,{\rm d}x-\frac{\kappa}{n-2}\int_{0}^{\frac{M}{\omega_{n}}}\frac{x}{r^{n-2}}\,{\rm d}x

\displaystyle\quad+\varepsilon\int_{0}^{\tau}\int_{0}^{\frac{M}{\omega_{n}}}p(\rho)\rho_{x}^{2}\,r^{2n-2}{\rm d}x{\rm d}s+\frac{1}{n}p(\rho(\tau,\frac{M}{\omega_{n}}))b(\tau)^{n}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{n\varepsilon}\int_{0}^{\tau}p(\rho(\tau,\frac{M}{\omega_{n}}))p^{\prime}(\rho(\tau,\frac{M}{\omega_{n}}))b(s)^{n}\,{\rm d}s

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\frac{M}{\omega_{n}}}\frac{1}{2}(u_{0}+\varepsilon r_{0}^{n-1}\rho_{0x})^{2}\,{\rm d}x+\int_{0}^{\frac{M}{\omega_{n}}}e(\rho_{0})\,{\rm d}x-\frac{\kappa}{n-2}\int_{0}^{\frac{M}{\omega_{n}}}\frac{x}{r_{0}^{n-2}}\,{\rm d}x

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{n}b^{n}p(\rho_{0}(\frac{M}{\omega_{n}}))+\varepsilon\kappa\int_{0}^{\tau}\int_{0}^{\frac{M}{\omega_{n}}}\rho\,{\rm d}x{\rm d}s-\varepsilon\kappa\frac{M}{\omega_{n}}\int_{0}^{\tau}\rho(s,\frac{M}{\omega_{n}})\,{\rm d}s.

Plugging (
3.55
) back to the Eulerian coordinates and using Lemma
3.1
, we obtain

(3.56)

\displaystyle\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{4}\int_{a}^{b(t)}\big|\big(\sqrt{\rho(t,r)}\big)_{r}\big|^{2}\,r^{n-1}{\rm d}r+\frac{4a_{0}\varepsilon}{\gamma}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{a}^{b(s)}\big|\big(\rho^{\frac{\gamma}{2}}\big)_{r}\big|^{2}\,r^{n-1}{\rm d}r{\rm d}s

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{n}p(\rho(t,b(t)))b(t)^{n}+\frac{1}{n\varepsilon}\int_{0}^{t}p(\rho(s,b(s)))p^{\prime}(\rho(s,b(s)))b(s)^{n}\,{\rm d}s

\displaystyle\leq C(E_{0},M)+\frac{1}{n}p(\rho_{0}(b))b^{n}+\varepsilon\kappa\int_{0}^{t}\int_{a}^{b(s)}\rho^{2}\,r^{n-1}{\rm d}r{\rm d}s

\displaystyle\quad-\varepsilon\kappa\frac{M}{\omega_{n}}\int_{0}^{t}\rho(s,b(s))\,{\rm d}s.

2. For the second term of (
3.56
)-RHS, it follows from (
3.12
) that

(3.57)

\frac{1}{n}p(\rho_{0}(b))b^{n}\leq C.

For the last term of (
3.56
)-RHS, by using (
3.47
), we have

(3.58)

\displaystyle\left|\varepsilon\kappa\frac{M}{\omega_{n}}\int_{0}^{t}\rho(s,b(s))\,{\rm d}s\right|\leq C(M)\rho_{0}(b)T\leq C(M,T).

3. For
\kappa=-1
(plasmas), then (
3.48
) follows from (
3.56
) and (
3.58
).

4. To close the estimates for
\kappa=1
(gaseous stars), we still need to bound the third term of (
3.56
)-RHS:

\varepsilon\kappa\int_{0}^{t}\int_{a}^{b(s)}\rho^{2}\,r^{n-1}{\rm d}r{\rm d}s=\frac{\varepsilon\kappa}{\omega_{n}}\int_{0}^{t}\|\rho(s)\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega_{s})}{\rm d}s.

We estimate the above term in the following two cases:

Case
1. For
\gamma\geq 2
, then it is bounded as

(3.59)

\displaystyle\varepsilon\kappa\int_{0}^{t}\int_{a}^{b(s)}\rho^{2}\,r^{n-1}{\rm d}r{\rm d}s

\displaystyle\leq C\varepsilon\int_{0}^{t}\int_{a}^{b(s)}\rho\big(1+e(\rho)\big)\,r^{n-1}{\rm d}r{\rm d}s

\displaystyle\leq C(E_{0},M,T).

Case
2. For
\gamma\in(\frac{2n}{n+2},2)
, we notice that
\frac{n\gamma}{n-2}>2
and use the interpolation inequality to obtain

(3.60)

\displaystyle\|\rho(t)\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{t})}\leq\|\rho(t)\|_{L^{\frac{n\gamma}{n-2}}(\Omega_{t})}^{\bar{\vartheta}}\,\|\rho(t)\|_{L^{\gamma}(\Omega_{t})}^{1-\bar{\vartheta}}\qquad\mbox{with $\bar{\vartheta}=\frac{n(2-\gamma)}{4}$}.

For
B_{R}(\mathbf{0})\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
, the following Sobolev inequality holds:

(3.61)

\displaystyle\|f\|_{L^{\frac{2n}{n-2}}(B_{R}(\mathbf{0}))}\leq C\big(\|\nabla f\|_{L^{2}(B_{R}(\mathbf{0}))}+\frac{1}{R}\|f\|_{L^{2}(B_{R}(\mathbf{0}))}\big).

It follows from (
3.16
) that

\displaystyle\frac{M}{\omega_{n}}

\displaystyle=\int_{a}^{b(t)}\rho(t,r)\,r^{n-1}{\rm d}r

\displaystyle\leq\Big(\int_{a}^{b(t)}\rho^{\gamma}\,r^{n-1}{\rm d}r\Big)^{\frac{1}{\gamma}}\Big(\int_{a}^{b(t)}r^{n-1}{\rm d}r\Big)^{1-\frac{1}{\gamma}}

\displaystyle\leq n^{\frac{1}{\gamma}-1}b(t)^{n(1-\frac{1}{\gamma})}\Big(\int_{a}^{b(t)}\rho^{\gamma}\,r^{n-1}{\rm d}r\Big)^{\frac{1}{\gamma}},

which yields that

(3.62)

\displaystyle b(t)^{-1}\leq\Big(n^{\frac{1-\gamma}{\gamma}}\,\frac{\omega_{n}}{M}\Big)^{\frac{\gamma}{n(\gamma-1)}}\Big(\int_{a}^{b(t)}\rho^{\gamma}\,r^{n-1}{\rm d}r\Big)^{\frac{1}{n(\gamma-1)}}\leq C(M,E_{0}).

This, together with Lemma
3.1
and (
3.61
), yields that

(3.63)

\displaystyle\|\rho(t)\|_{L^{\frac{n\gamma}{n-2}}(\Omega_{t})}

\displaystyle=\Big(\|\rho^{\frac{\gamma}{2}}(t)\|_{L^{\frac{2n}{n-2}}(\Omega_{t})}\Big)^{\frac{2}{\gamma}}

\displaystyle\leq C\Big(\|\nabla(\rho^{\frac{\gamma}{2}})\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{t})}+b(t)^{-1}\|\rho^{\frac{\gamma}{2}}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{t})}\Big)^{\frac{2}{\gamma}}

\displaystyle\leq C(M,E_{0})\Big(1+\big(\int_{a}^{b(t)}|(\rho^{\frac{\gamma}{2}})_{r}|^{2}r^{n-1}{\rm d}r\big)^{\frac{1}{\gamma}}\Big).

Substituting (
3.63
) into (
3.60
) and using (
3.1
) and the Hölder inequality, we have

(3.64)

\displaystyle\varepsilon\kappa\int_{0}^{t}\int_{a}^{b(s)}\rho^{2}\,r^{n-1}{\rm d}r{\rm d}s

\displaystyle\leq C(M,E_{0})\int_{0}^{t}\varepsilon\Big(1+\big(\int_{a}^{b(s)}|(\rho^{\frac{\gamma}{2}})_{r}|^{2}r^{n-1}{\rm d}r\big)^{\frac{1}{\gamma}}\Big)^{2\bar{\vartheta}}{\rm d}s

\displaystyle\leq C(M,E_{0},T)+\frac{\varepsilon a_{0}}{\gamma}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{a}^{b(s)}|(\rho^{\frac{\gamma}{2}})_{r}|^{2}\,r^{n-1}{\rm d}r{\rm d}s,

where we have used
\displaystyle\frac{2\bar{\vartheta}}{\gamma}\in(0,1)
for
\gamma>\frac{2n}{n+2}
.
Finally, combining (
3.56
)–(
3.57
), (
3.59
), and (
3.64
), we obtain (
3.48
).
This completes the proof.
∎

From (
3.62
), we know that
b(t)
has a uniform positive lower bound.
However, to take limit
b\rightarrow\infty
,
we need to make sure that domain
\Omega_{T}
can expand to the whole physical space for fixed
\varepsilon>0
; that is,

\displaystyle\inf_{t\in[0,T]}b(t)\longrightarrow\infty
as
b\rightarrow\infty
.

Lemma 3.4

(Expanding of domain
\Omega_{T}
)

Given
T>0
and
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0}]
, there exists a positive constant
C_{1}(M,E_{0},T,\varepsilon)>0
such that,
if
\displaystyle b\geq\max\{C_{1}(M,E_{0},T,\varepsilon),\mathfrak{B}(\varepsilon)\}
,

(3.65)

\displaystyle b(t)\geq\frac{b}{2}\qquad\,\,\mbox{for $t\in[0,T]$},

where
\mathfrak{B}(\varepsilon)
is defined for (
A.42
).

Proof.

Noting the continuity of
b(t)
, we first make the
a priori
assumption:

(3.66)

b(t)\geq\frac{b}{4}.

Integrating (
3.3
) over
[0,t]
yields

(3.67)

\displaystyle b(t)=b+\int_{0}^{t}u(s,b(s))\,{\rm d}s.

A direct calculation by using (
3.47
), (
3.66
), and Lemma
3.1
yields that

(3.68)

\displaystyle\left|\int_{0}^{t}u(s,b(s))\,{\rm d}s\right|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}\Big(\int_{0}^{t}\varepsilon(\rho u^{2}r^{n-2})(s,b(s))\,{\rm d}s\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}}\Big(\int_{0}^{t}\frac{1}{\rho(s,b(s))b(s)^{n-2}}{\rm d}s\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C(M,E_{0})}{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}\Big(\int_{0}^{t}\frac{1}{\rho(s,b(s))b(s)^{n-2}}{\rm d}s\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{C(M,E_{0})}{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}\bigg(\int_{0}^{t}\frac{\big(1+\frac{(\gamma-1)^{2}}{\varepsilon}e(\rho_{0}(b))s\big)^{\frac{1}{\gamma-1}}}{\rho_{0}(b)b(s)^{n-2}}{\rm d}s\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\leq C_{0}(M,E_{0})\,\Big(\frac{1+T}{\varepsilon}\Big)^{\frac{\gamma}{2(\gamma-1)}}\rho_{0}(b)^{-\frac{1}{2}}b^{-\frac{n-2}{2}}.

We take
